\inlinenote{Make sure to clearly articulate that all animal motion, brute and rational, is prompted by desire. Therefore, any animal motion is the actualization of desire. Aristotle treats animal locomotion as proceeding jointly from practical thought and appetite, with the desired object as the unmoved mover that sets the chain in motion (\cite{aristotle2014soul} III.10, 433a13--15, 433a17--20; \textit{De Motu Animalium} 6, 700b15--22): ``Both thought and appetite are capable of originating local movement: practical thought\ldots\ differs from speculative thought'' (\textit{DA} III.10, 433a13--15); ``all living things both move and are moved for the sake of something'' (\textit{MA} 6, 700b15--17); ``wish, however, impulse, and appetite, are all three forms of desire'' (\textit{MA} 6, 700b22--23). \textit{DA} III.10 establishes the \textit{orektikon--dianoia praktikē} coupling; \textit{MA} 6 supplies the species-level catalogue of \textit{orexis} into \textit{epithymia}, \textit{thymos}, and \textit{boulēsis}. Both loci are required to underwrite the introduction's framing of animal motion as desire-actualization.}

\inlinenote{``There is, then, something which is always moved with an unceasing motion, which is motion in a circle; and this is plain not in theory only but in fact. Therefore the first heavens must be eternal. There is therefore also something which moves them. And since that which is moved and moves is intermediate, there is a mover which moves without being moved, being eternal, substance, and actuality. And the object of desire and the object of thought move in this way: they move without being moved. The primary objects of desire and of thought are the same. For the apparent good is the object of appetite, and the real good is the primary object of wish. But desire is consequent on opinion rather than opinion on desire: for the thinking is the starting-point'' (\textit{Metaphysica} XII.7, 1072a21--29).}

\section{Rhetorical \textit{Phantasia}: The Soul's Temporal Medium}

In his \textit{De Anima}, Aristotle's central task is to, essentially, answer the question ``What is the soul (\textit{psychē}), and what does it do?'' As he famously stated: ``[t]he knowledge of the soul admittedly contributes greatly to the advance of truth in general, and, above all, to our understanding of Nature, for the soul is in some sense the principle of animal life'' (I.1, 402a5--7). In pursuit of this inquiry, Aristotle will seek to define the essence of the soul, analyze its different faculties,\footnote{According to Aristotle's \textit{De Anima}, these faculties consist of the nutritive (II.2, 413a23--b1), sensitive (II.3, 414a30--b1; II.5, 416b32--417a28), appetitive (III.9-11, 432a15-434a21), locomotive (III.10--11, 433a12--434a15), imaginative (III.3-4, 427b7-429a9), and intellect or reason (III.4--8, esp. 429a13--431b20).} explain how the soul relates to the body,\footnote{Aristotle argues, rejecting Plato's dualism, that the soul is not a separate immaterial entity, but as the form or organizing principle that gives the body its life-function (II.1, 412a20--26, 412a27--28, 412b6--9, 414a14--21; II.4, 415b9--12).} and to understand perception, imagination, and thought. It is in this account that we find Aristotle most clearly engaging with the relationship between the soul and the senses. He will demonstrate how \textit{phantasia}\footnote{Papachristou insists on leaving the Greek word \textit{phantasia} untranslated precisely because its standard English rendering as ``imagination'' as well as the overall semantic breadth of the term obscures its tripartite ambiguity---its simultaneous reference to capacity, activity, and product (28n32). Indeed, to translate \textit{phantasia} simply as ``imagination'' would collapse the ontological distinction between the soul's active power of image-formation and the image that stands before the mind as a result of that power. Aristotle's account requires us to recognize that \textit{phantasia} admits of internal grades: specifically, the distinction between sensory \textit{phantasia}, which operates in all animals possessing perception, and deliberative \textit{phantasia}, which belongs only to rational animals capable of calculation and planning. Accordingly, in line with Papachristou, I too will leave \textit{phantasia} untranslated throughout.} plays an integral role in psychic processes like perception, cognition, deliberation, interpretation, and bears centrally on \textit{phantasia}'s function in human experience. The question of \textit{phantasia}---what it is, how it functions, and why Aristotle accords it such a pivotal role in the architecture of the soul---is one of the most crucial elements underpinning much of Aristotle's psychology, though, perhaps, one of the most challenging to pin down. Indeed, the difficulty arises not merely from the semantic breadth of the term, which resists any simple translation,\footnote{Aristotelian \textit{phantasia}, as noted by Debra Hawhee, is most often translated as ``imagination'' or ``mental imagery'' (141). Dorothea Frede argues that \textit{phantasia} serves three functions: ``It designates the capacity, the activity or process, and the product or result.'' Later on, she posits that ```appearance' in a wider sense should be regarded as the central meaning to which all the functions of the term are related. It would then be (i) the capacity to experience an appearance, (ii) the on-going appearance itself, and (iii) what appears'' (279).} but from the structural position \textit{phantasia} occupies between \textit{aisthēsis} (direct sensation) and \textit{noēsis} (thought), between the body's reception of sensible forms and the soul's capacity to deliberate, judge, act, and feel.

As Aristotle defines it, \textit{phantasia} ``is that in virtue of which an image arises for us,'' it is ``a faculty or disposition relative to images, in virtue of which we discriminate and are either in error or not'' (\textit{DA} III.3, 428a1--3). Elsewhere, Aristotle describes \textit{phantasia} as being fundamental for thinking: ``[t]o the thinking soul images serve as if they were contents of perception (and when it asserts or denies them to be good or bad it avoids or pursues them). That is why the soul never thinks without an image'' (III.7, 431a15--17). Furthermore, as Aristotle explains shortly after, ``sometimes by means of the images or thoughts which are within the soul, just as if it were seeing, it calculates and deliberates what is to come by reference to what is present'' (III.7, 431b6--8). ``This mental calculation,'' Ned O'Gorman remarks, ``consists of `combination' (\textit{sumplokē}) and is essential to the formation of opinion (\textit{doxa}) through practical reasoning and the realization of truth (\textit{alēthēs}) through contemplative thought'' (20).

\textit{Phantasia}, therefore, functions to mediate sense perception and deliberative or speculative thinking. It, as Martha Nussbaum articulates, ``ties abstract thought to concrete perceptible objects or situations'' (\textit{Phantasia} 265). \textit{Phantasia} is presented in \textit{De Anima} as being fundamental to human thought and mental operations; however, as Aristotle is careful to articulate, \textit{phantasia} is not ``any of the things that are never in error: e.g. knowledge or intelligence; for imagination may be false'' (III.3, 428a16--18). In this sense, \textit{phantasia} is active when we see a hazy figure in the distance and believe it to be a man. Yet we may well find out, as we come closer to the figure in question, that it is nothing more than a small tree. All the images brought to the mind by \textit{phantasia} have their origin in \textit{aisthēsis} (direct sense perception), and Aristotle describes \textit{phantasia} as a movement causally related to direct perception: ``imagination must be a movement resulting from an actual exercise of a power of sense'' (III.3, 429a2). However, \textit{phantasia} is activated not when we perceive something distinctly, ``but rather when there is some failure of accuracy in its [\textit{aisthēsis}'] exercise'' (III.3, 428a14--15). It supplies what is missing when the object cannot be perceived or when it is not definitively perceptible. While \textit{phantasia} is a distinct capacity of the mind and differs from both thought and perception, ``it is not found without sensation, or judgment without it'' (III.3, 427b15).

Kevin White elucidates \textit{phantasia} through its functional role within the soul's cognitive architecture. White emphasizes that \textit{phantasia} serves as an intermediary between \textit{aisthēsis} and \textit{nous}, facilitating the transition from the immediate reception of sensory data to the abstract processes of reasoning and deliberation. White highlights that \textit{phantasia} is characterized as a movement resulting from actual sensation, persisting even after the sensory experience has ceased. This persistence allows for the retention and manipulation of sensory impressions (\textit{phantasmata}), which are essential for memory, imagination, and the deliberative aspects of thought.\footnote{Throughout this dissertation, I observe Aristotle's distinction between \textit{phantasia} (the faculty or capacity for image-making) and \textit{phantasma} (the determinate image-product or appearance generated by that faculty); the plural \textit{phantasmata} denotes a manifold of such products.} ``Once having gained a foothold in time by means of \textit{phantasia},'' White concludes, ``\textit{nous} is capable of putting its survey of the past into the service of a calculation of the future. The importance of experience for practical wisdom is clearly based on the preserving activity of \textit{phantasia}'' (502). In other words, \textit{phantasia's} ability to store impressions of our interactions with objects of sense provides the necessary content upon which the intellect operates, and allows us to understand our present circumstances and project future anticipations in view of our previous sensory experiences. \textit{Phantasia}, then, serves to rhetorically mediate sensorial life ``just as if it were seeing, it calculates and plans for the future in view of the present'' and the past, transmuting the content of experience into images (O'Gorman 21). As noted by White, Frede, and O'Gorman, in accordance with this causal relationship, \textit{phantasia} functions as the faculty responsible for the retention of sense perceptions or what Frede calls ``after-images'' (\textit{Phantasia} 280). Thanks to this function, \textit{phantasia} encompasses recollection, dreaming, visualizations, and imaginings. Furthermore, Aristotle seems to suggest that at least certain emotional responses depend on the \textit{phantasmatic} capacity of the soul. This is most clearly illustrated in Aristotle's definition of fear in the \textit{Rhetorica}: ``[f]ear may be defined as a pain or disturbance due to \textit{imagining} some destructive or painful evil in the future'' (\textit{Rhetorica} II.5, 1382a22--23; my emphasis).

From these descriptions and interpretations, we can see that \textit{phantasia} plays a fundamental role in recollection, imaginings and dreams, deliberative and speculative thought (of past, present, and future), the formation of opinion or \textit{doxa}, and is even connected to the ability of emotion or affect to evoke movement. However, I believe the ontological and phenomenological implications evoked by Aristotle's notion of \textit{phantasia} are far more fundamental to the human experience of reality. ``In the noetic or human soul,'' White observes, ``\textit{phantasia} is a step in man's progression beyond time altogether, enabling him at certain points and for certain periods to pass beyond the confinements of sense to a consideration of the timeless nature of things'' (503). I think White makes a crucial observation in noting the fundamental relation between time and \textit{phantasia}. I believe Aristotle's concept of \textit{phantasia}---as a fundamental dynamic process of the human soul---lies at the core of human being through its capacity to create a continuous narrative of experience, enabling humans to navigate their reality cohesively in time.

\textit{Phantasia}'s distinctive ontological role lies in its temporal range: it stretches the soul's activity across past, present, and future, and is the medium through which all three temporal horizons remain available to the higher-order operations of the soul. With respect to the past, \textit{phantasia} retains prior perceptual encounters as \textit{phantasmata} accessible as the material upon which memory and recollection operate. With respect to the present, \textit{phantasia} is the resonant medium of the ongoing perceptual \textit{taking-as}: the kinetic continuation through which sensorial experience persists, can be re-presented, and offers itself to thought, opinion, emotion, and the soul's higher-order awareness of its own perceiving. With respect to the future, \textit{phantasia} is the projective faculty through which the soul calculates, anticipates, deliberates, and forms the images that draw it toward what is to come; without \textit{phantasia} there is no calculation by reference to what is present, no deliberative composition of \textit{phantasmata}, and no desirable projection toward a realizable good. The soul's higher-order operations---intellection, opinion-formation, memory and recollection, deliberative and speculative thought, the emotions, and the practical reasoning that issues in action---unfold across these three temporal horizons rather than within any one of them; each requires holding-together what is absent (past or future) with what is present, and that holding-together is the work \textit{phantasia} accomplishes. \textit{Phantasia} is, therefore, not merely instrumental to the soul's higher operations but constitutive of their possibility: it is what makes the soul a being whose cognitive and affective life is temporally distributed rather than confined to the immediacy of the live perceptual encounter.\footnote{Across all three temporal registers, \textit{phantasia} subtends the higher-order operations: thought, opinion, memory, deliberation, and the emotions all operate upon an object held across time rather than received in the immediacy of the live percipient-perceptible co-actualization alone, and it is \textit{phantasia} that secures that holding-across.} 

\begin{comment}
    The following was place before the start of the footnote which ends the previous paragraph: The temporal range developed here gathers three converging sets of Aristotelian texts. (i) \textit{Past}: \textit{phantasia} retains prior perception as the precondition for memory and recollection: ``memory is the having of an image [\textit{phantasma}] related as a likeness [\textit{eikōn}] to that of which it is an image'' (\textit{De Memoria et Reminiscentia} 1, 451a15--18); memory is ``neither perception nor conception, but a state or affection of one of these, conditioned by lapse of time'' (1, 449b24--25); ``[t]he process of movement stamps in, as it were, a sort of impression of the percept, just as persons do who make an impression with a seal'' (1, 450a31--b1). Frede captures the function as the \textit{phantasmata} that operate as ``after-images'' (\textit{Phantasia} 280). (ii) \textit{Present---resonant kinēsis and the live taking}: live \textit{aisthēsis} is the joint actualization of percipient and perceptible (\cite{aristotle2014soul} II.5, 416b32--417a20; III.2, 425b26: ``the actualization of the sensible object and that of the percipient sense is one and the same actualization, though their being is not the same''), and that actualization is conjoint with the soul's higher-order awareness of its own perceiving (\cite{aristotle2014soul} III.2, 425b12--25). The persisting \textit{resonant kinēsis} that issues from this live encounter is what \textit{phantasia} continues to operate upon once the live object withdraws (\cite{aristotle2014soul} III.3, 429a1--2: \textit{phantasia} is ``a movement resulting from an actual exercise of a power of sense''). (iii) \textit{Future---calculation, deliberation, anticipation}: \textit{phantasia} is the medium through which the soul orients itself to what is to come: ``sometimes by means of the images or thoughts which are within the soul, just as if it were seeing, it calculates and deliberates what is to come by reference to what is present'' (\cite{aristotle2014soul} III.7, 431b6--8); ``the soul never thinks without an image'' (\cite{aristotle2014soul} III.7, 431b2); and the deliberative grade of \textit{phantasia} (\textit{bouleutikē}) is what enables the human soul to compose multiple \textit{phantasmata} into unified objects of practical reasoning (\cite{aristotle2014soul} III.11, 434a5--10). White's reading consolidates the future-orientation: ``Once having gained a foothold in time by means of \textit{phantasia}, \textit{nous} is capable of putting its survey of the past into the service of a calculation of the future'' (\cite{white1985meaning} 502). 
\end{comment}

What distinguishes \textit{phantasia} from \textit{aisthēsis} on the one hand and \textit{noēsis} on the other is precisely its temporal character---its capacity to hold and reproduce what is no longer present, to make the absent sensorially available.\footnote{Aristotle marks off \textit{phantasia} from perception, opinion, and discursive thought across \textit{De Anima} III.3: ``Imagination is different from either perceiving or discursive thinking, though it is not found without sensation, or judgement without it'' (427b14--16); imagination ``takes place in the absence of'' direct sense (428a6--7); and ``imagination may be false,'' but knowledge and intelligence are never in error (428a16--18). The cluster's central feature is the temporal mode-of-operation: \textit{phantasia} is the faculty that operates in the absence of the live object, retaining and reproducing what was perceived but is no longer present.} \textit{Phantasia} is not a mixture of sensation and judgment, but a distinct mode of disclosure rooted in the soul's \textit{kinetic} constitution.\footnote{Aristotle explicitly resists a mixture model: \textit{phantasia} is ``not found without sensation'' but is also not the same as sensation (cf. \textit{DA} III.3, 428b10--429a9). The \textit{phantasia}'s constitution is \textit{kinetic}---a residual movement caused by actual perception---not a combination of two prior faculties. The distinction is decisive: ``when we \textit{think} something to be fearful or threatening, emotion is immediately produced ... but when we merely \textit{imagine} we remain as unaffected as persons who are looking at a painting of some dreadful or encouraging scene'' (427b21--24). The contrast presupposes that \textit{phantasia} and \textit{nous} (thinking) are not parts of one mixed operation but distinct \textit{kinetic} structures.} 
 
 Thus, \textit{phantasia} is neither a mere residue of sensation nor an autonomous cognitive act; rather, it is the mediating power through which sensory motion persists, transforms, and becomes available for higher-order operations of the soul.\footnote{Victor Caston's framing of why Aristotle introduces \textit{phantasia} as a separate faculty makes the mediating-power claim structurally explicit: ``\textit{Phantasia} thus stands midway between these different powers. It is more basic than the intellectual powers, extending throughout the animal kingdom, while also being capable of error. In this way, it is ideally suited to explain the errors that are manifest in the behavior of even nonrational animals. Since it does not require conviction, it bears content in a more neutral way and so provide the basis for the content of a wide variety of mental states'' (46).} For now, it suffices to establish the ontological ground for which I will be arguing: \textit{phantasia} is the soul's temporal medium, the faculty through which motion becomes \textit{phantasmata}, and \textit{phantasmata} become the material of thought (and emotion). However, to understand \textit{phantasia} adequately, we must first situate it within the broader ontological framework of motion and time: the very conditions under which anything can appear, endure, and recur within the soul's experience.

From Aristotle's account of sensation through the various functions of \textit{phantasia} there is a guiding principle which connects all the functions of and temporal realms within which \textit{phantasia} operates: \textit{being-in-motion-and-time}. Therefore, beginning with his account of sensation---on which \textit{phantasia} depends---and working through the temporal components and functions, I will argue that Aristotle's conception of \textit{phantasia} is best understood as the temporal and kinetic capacity that generates a continuous, affectively charged narrative of experience, enabling human beings to cohesively navigate, interpret, and reshape their reality across past, present, and future. Anchored in this principle of \textit{being-in-motion-and-time}, \textit{phantasia} marks the pivotal transitional process whereby the world, through sensation, enters the soul as \textit{kinetic} and affective resonance, and whereby the soul, through the activation of \textit{phantasmata}, becomes capable of internally navigating, recombining, and imaginatively transforming that world. To influence someone's \textit{phantasia} is thus to shape not merely their beliefs, but the very structure of their perceptual and affective reality---altering how they experience, interpret, and anticipate the world as an integrated, temporally unfolding whole.\footnote{Daniel Gross's \textit{Uncomfortable Situations} develops a contemporary articulation of this rhetoric's world-shaping thesis under the rubric of rhetorical affordance: ``[r]hetoric, as a humanistic form of affordance theory, posits a situation that is not neutral but is rather `persuasive,' threatening and promising with respect to our human being-in-the-world. More precisely rhetorical theory provides a set of awarenesses specially appropriate to human environments ...'' (93). 

The Aristotelian \textit{phantasia} I am developing as the \textit{kinetic}-temporal medium of perceptual-affective reality is the cognitive-architectural condition for the kind of rhetorical-affordance dynamics Gross specifies at the contemporary register.} Indeed, in shaping the very structure of our perceptual and affective reality, \textit{phantasia}, as I will argue, lies at the heart of humanity's rhetorical being-in-the-world: a notion which understands our existence as being inextricably intertwined with the world around us. For ``rhetoric stems from and is not imposed on the world. Rhetoric,'' as Thomas Rickert argues, ``is ambient and cannot be confined to a human orbit'' (\textit{Ambient}~285).


\subsection{The Architecture of Actualization: Schema, Chain, Iteration}

\textit{Phantasia} is not, for Aristotle, a peripheral faculty exercised only in daydreams or bringing images before the eyes; it is the faculty upon which every waking act of cognition depends, for ``the soul never thinks without an image'' (\textit{DA} III.7, 431a17), and the image that thought operates upon is always furnished by \textit{phantasia}. Its architectural role in everyday experience is yet more fundamental: insofar as \textit{phantasia}, as I will argue, is the temporal medium through which the soul's successive perceptual instants are held together as a single cohesive narrative, binding the remembered past, the attended present, and the anticipated future into the continuous temporal horizon within which human life---thinking, deliberating, remembering, and acting---unfolds. The soul is not, on the reading I will develop, a cognitive faculty that subsequently enters into rhetorical or affective relations with its world; it is constituted, from the moment of its existential being, as a being whose kinetic and affective dimensions are constitutive rather than supplementary.\footnote{This anti-internalist framing is most pointedly articulated by Heidegger in his 1924 Marburg lectures: ``the \gk{πάθη} [\textit{pathē}] are not ``psychic experiences,'' are not ``in consciousness,'' but are a being-taken of human beings in their full being-in-the-world.'' (\textit{BCAP} 133). ``To say that the soul gets angry,'' Heidegger states quoting Aristotle, ``is the same as wanting to say that the soul builds a house. it would be better to say not that the soul has pity or learns or believes something, but that the human being does \gk{τῇ ψυχῇ} [\textit{tē psychē}, ``with the soul''].'' Soul is here conceived as \gk{οὐσία} [\textit{ousia}, ``substance,'' ``being,'' or ``essence''], insofar as the being-taken of beings as living things is expressed in the \gk{πάθη}.'' (133). \textit{Pathos} thus names a ``\textit{being-taken} of being-there. Being-there is taken with that which is there in the world with being-there itself---\textit{from without}, but from without \textit{in the sense of the world as the wherein of my being}.'' (132-133). The reading I develop here adopts this line as foundational: the affective and kinetic structure of the soul is not an addition to a prior internal cognitive subject (``I think therefore I am''), but the mode through which the soul, as being-in-the-world is what it is.} The capacity for being-affected is not a secondary feature added to cognition but a co-equal articulation of the very same actuality that cognition itself articulates, given under a different \textit{logos}: where cognition articulates the actuality as the soul's grasping of what is, being-affected articulates the same actuality as the soul's being-moved by what is. The two are not sequenced operations stacked on a neutral cognitive substrate but two \textit{logoi}---two structural accounts---of one indivisible moment in which the soul, as being-in-the-world, is at once cognitively grasping and affectively taken.\footnote{The claim that cognition and being-affected articulate the same underlying actuality under different \textit{logoi} is drawn from two distinct Aristotelian claims: (1) The co-given actualization of perceiver and the perceptible: ``[t]he activity of the sensible object and that of sense is one and the same activity, and yet the distinction between their being remains'' (\textit{DA} III.2, 425b26--27). The qualifier ``distinction between their being remains'' names precisely the structural possibility that one actuality admits of two distinct accounts: the same actualization, articulated as the perceptible's becoming actually perceived (the cognitive \textit{logos}), is at once articulated as the percipient's become actually affected (the affective \textit{logos}). (2) The higher-order awareness of perception: ``[s]ince it is through sense that we are aware that we are seeing or hearing, it must be either by sight that we are aware of seeing, or by some sense other than sight. But the sense that gives us this new sensation must perceive both sight and its object, viz. colour: so that either there will be two senses both percipient of the same sensible object, or the sense must be percipient of itself. Further, even if the sense which perceives sight were different from sight, we must either fall into an infinite regress, or we must somewhere assume a sense which is aware of itself'' (III.2, 425b11-17). Awareness of perceiving is not a separate operation appended to perception but a further articulation of the perceptual actuality itself: the soul's reflexive grasp of its own being-affected. Taken together, Aristotle demonstrates that cognition and being-affected are not sequenced operations stacked on a neutral cognitive substrate but two articulations (\textit{logoi}) of one structural moment in which the soul, as being-in-the-world, is at once cognitively grasping and affectively taken by what discloses itself to it.} For the present section's purposes, the framing supplies the motivating stakes: the chain illustrating the unfolding from perception-to-action and, what I call, the actualization of desire, is not a metaphysically neutral apparatus but the structure through which the rhetorically constituted soul unfolds its perceptual and appetitive capacities in engagement with a world that matters to it. What this section traces is the architectural sequence through which that mattering is generated, with \textit{phantasia} standing at its temporal center as the mode of disclosure that sustains the cohesive narrative within which every subsequent motion the chain details becomes intelligible. 

However, before we can analyze \textit{phantasia}'s operations and perceptual motion in general, we must first establish the ontological framework within which all such operations, perception, and all coming-to-be and passing-away take place. Accordingly, this section advances a sustained interpretive reading of Aristotle's account of \textit{aisthēsis} (direct sense-perception), \textit{phantasia} (imagination), cognition, and appetitive action as a single architectural sequence: as a progressive chain of actualizations through which the journey from sensible object to completed action is depicted as a series of iterated motions linking completed actualities. Each node in the chain is an actuality; each link is a motion; and each motion produces the actuality that the next motion will presuppose. This actualization chain is not given by Aristotle explicitly; rather, it is my interpretive construction, a unified architectural reading, that integrates his analyses scattered across multiple treatises---\textit{De Anima}, \textit{Physica}, \textit{Metaphysica}, \textit{De Sensu et Sensibilibus}, \textit{De Memoria et Reminiscentia}, \textit{De Insomniis}, \textit{De Motu Animalium}, and the \textit{Rhetorica}---and is strongly informed by the thinking of Martin Heidegger and, to a lesser degree, others. The present section details the methodological apparatus that makes that reading possible: the three-factor kinetic schema, the priority of actuality, the structural principle that each motion is named by its terminus, the scale-invariance of relative unmoved originators, and the interlocking of actuality-first architecture with the iterated chain. While each of these elements will be treated in greater depth in their respective sections, I will briefly discuss them here to lay the groundwork for my analysis of the actualization chain that follows. 

The actualization chain advanced here (See Figure ***) organizes Aristotle's analyses of perception and action into five principal nodes: $A_0$ (the dyadic first actuality of the sensible object and the faculty of sense, situated within motion and time as the encompassing horizon), $A_1$ (completed perception---the joint actualization of the sensible object's active potency with the sense-organ's receptive potency, together with the dual residual tone it deposits in the rational soul), $A_2$ (the \textit{phantasma} proper, generated from the \textit{resonant kinēsis} deposited at $A_1$), $A_3$ (the completed cognitive actuality---the \textit{phantasma} engaged by the three orientational modes of intellection, memory, and discursive thinking, together with the orthogonal committal dimension of \textit{doxa} and subsequent emotion), and $A_4$ (the completed action, achieved when the \textit{orektikon}\footnote{Throughout this dissertation I retain \textit{orexis} and its three species---\textit{epithymia}, \textit{thymos}, and \textit{boulēsis}---in Greek, untranslated. \textit{Orektikon} names the faculty of \textit{orexis}. While I maintain the use of the Greek throughout, I understand \textit{orexis} as desire (the overall faculty, genus) consisting of three species: \textit{epithymia} (appetite---bodily, pursue the pleasant/good, avoid the painful/bad), \textit{thymos} (spirit---honor-defense, ambition, retaliation), and \textit{boulēsis} (rational wish---deliberative orientation toward the good). The English convention is inconsistent across Aristotelian translations in the Revised Oxford Translation: J.\,A.\ Smith's \textit{De Anima} renders \textit{orexis} as ``appetite'' and \textit{epithymia} as ``desire'' (DA II.3, 414b2), while Forster's \textit{De Motu Animalium} (700b22--23) and Roberts's \textit{Rhetorica} (1370a16--19) use the opposite English convention. Aristotle's own usage supports taking \textit{orexis} (\gk{ὄρεξις}) as the genus of desire and \textit{epithymia} (\gk{ἐπιθυμία}, ``appetite''), \textit{thymos} (\gk{θυμὸς}, ``spirit''), and \textit{boulēsis} (\gk{βούλησις}, ``wish'') as the three species, rather than taking \textit{epithymia} as the encompassing genus. He states explicitly: ``\gk{ὄρεξις μὲν γὰρ ἐπιθυμία καὶ θυμὸς καὶ βούλησις}'' (De Anima 414b2–3, ed. Ross)  (\textit{orexis men gar epithymia kai thymos kai boulēsis}, ``for desire is appetite and spirit and wish''), thereby presenting \textit{orexis} as the encompassing term under which \textit{epithymia}, \textit{thymos}, and \textit{boulēsis} fall. He then defines \textit{epithymia} more narrowly as ``\gk{καὶ ἡ ἐπιθυμία τοῦ γὰρ ἡδέος ὄρεξις αὕτη}'' (\textit{kai hē epithymia tou gar hēdeos orexis hautē}, ``and appetite also for this is a desire for the pleasant''), that is, \textit{epithymia} (appetite) as the desire for the pleasant. This seemingly confirms that \textit{epithymia} is one determinate species of \textit{orexis}, and not the whole genus of desire. The same generic usage appears in \textit{Rhetorica}, where Aristotle says that all actions caused by oneself are due either ``\gk{δι' ἦθος}'' (\textit{di’ ēthos}, ``because of habit'') of ``\gk{δι' ὄρεξιν}'' (\textit{di’ orexin}, ``because of desire''). He then immediately differentiates \textit{orexis} into rational and irrational forms: ``\gk{ἔστι δ' ἡ μὲν βούλησις ἀγαθοῦ ὄρεξις ... ἄλογοι δ' ὀρέξεις ὀργὴ καὶ ἐπιθυμία}'' (\textit{esti d’ hē men boulēsis agathou orexis ... alogoi d’ orexeis orgē kai epithymia}, ``wishing is desire for good ... irrational desires are anger and appetite'') (I.10, 1368a37--1369a4; see \textit{Ars Rhetorica} ed. Kassel for Greek). Translator-side English appears verbatim only in quoted passages. } converges the cognitive actuality at $A_3$ into bodily movement). The chain admits three entry-points reflecting Aristotle's three elicitors of movement\footnote{``I want to drink, says appetite; this is drink, says \textit{sense} [\textit{aisthēsis}] or \textit{imagination} [\textit{phantasia}] or \textit{thought} [\textit{nous}]: straightaway I drink'' (\textit{MA} 7, 701a32--33; my emphasis).}: ``sense'' (\textit{aisthēsis}) enters at $A_1$ (the sensible object actualizes a fresh perception); ``imagination'' (\textit{phantasia}) enters at $A_2$ (an already-actualized \textit{phantasma} from prior sense is re-engaged); ``thought'' (\textit{nous}) enters at $A_3$ (an already-actualized cognitive content is engaged inferentially). All three issue in $A_4$ via the \textit{orektikon} motion. Here it must be noted that the movement or procession of each completed actualization represents not a series of discrete events within a series of discrete temporal moments but a continuous unfolding in which each stage is the necessary condition for the next,\footnote{The continuous-unfolding depends on two Aristotelian anchors: (a) Aristotle's definition of motion as ``the fulfilment [\textit{entelecheia}] of what is potentially, as such, is motion'' (\textit{Physica} III.1, 201a10--11)---the \textit{energeia} of the potential \textit{qua} potential, which is the very form of incomplete (\textit{ateles}) actuality and so secures the in-between status of the procession; and (b) Aristotle's grounding of time as ``the number of motion in respect of the before and after'' (\textit{Physica} IV.11, 219b1--2). The pair is what makes the procession-as-continuous-unfolding Aristotelian rather than a free-standing construction: each completed actualization is the terminus of an \textit{energeia ateles}, and the temporal measure of that \textit{energeia} is itself derivative from the motion it numbers.} much as Heidegger's analysis of temporality reveals Dasein's Being as always already stretched along (\textit{Erstreckung}) between past, present, and future.\footnote{See \textit{Being and Time} §72 and §65 on ecstatic-horizonal temporality. The stretching-along (\textit{Erstreckung}) of Dasein between birth and death is articulated as the unity of the ecstatic temporal horizons at \textit{Being and Time} §72: ``Dasein does not fill up a track or stretch `of life'---one which is somehow present-at-hand---with the phases of its momentary actualities. It stretches \textit{itself} along in such a way that its own Being is constituted in advance as a stretching-along'' (\textit{BT} 426). The earlier ecstatic-horizonal account at §65 (``Temporality `is' not an \textit{entity} at all. It is not, but it \textit{temporalizes} itself.... We therefore call the phenomena of the future, the character of having been, and the Present, the ``\textit{ecstases}'' of temporality.'' (377); this self temporalization is what grounds the stretching-along (\textit{Erstreckung}): the unity of the horizons is precisely what makes the stretching-along possible.}

\inlinenote{Either add Heidegger's translation of the different species of desire, and \textit{prohairesis} to the footnote, or mention in the footnote that it is further defined in the glossary with a page to glossary.}

This actualization chain, to reiterate, is my interpretive construction in the sense that no single Aristotelian treatise presents it as such. Aristotle supplies the components: the three-factor kinetic schema,\footnote{\textit{De Anima} III.10, 433b12--25.} the definition of motion,\footnote{\textit{Physica} III.1, 201a10--14.} the definition of time,\footnote{\textit{Physica} IV.11, 219b1--2.} the priority-of-actuality doctrine,\footnote{\textit{Metaphysica} IX.8, 1049b4--1050a16.} the hylomorphic account of perception,\footnote{\textit{De Anima} II.12, 424a17--24.} the treatment of residual motion,\footnote{\textit{De Insomniis} 2, 459a24--460b3; \textit{De Anima} III.3, 428b10--429a8.} the dependence of thought on images,\footnote{\textit{De Anima} III.7, 431a15--17 and III.8, 432a3--9.} the enmattered-accounts framework,\footnote{\textit{De Anima} I.1, 403a3--b19.} and the analysis of action from desire,\footnote{\textit{De Motu Animalium} 7, 701a7--701b1.}. The architectural work carried out in the rest of this section will be to integrate these into a sustained reading of the actualization of desire (perception-to-action) as a single iterated motion-structure, and to advance several interpretive readings that go beyond what Aristotle explicitly states: the ontological incompleteness of time without soul, the dual-tone thesis, the orthogonal committal dimension of \textit{doxa}, and the \textit{doxastic} ratification for emotion as articulated in Aristotle's \textit{Rhetorica}. 

\subsection{The Kinetic Schema: A Three-Factor Structure of Motion}

\textit{Phantasia}, as I will demonstrate later, ontologically depends on \textit{aisthēsis} (direct sense-perception) but in an asymmetrical relationship; it is, as Aristotle states, ``impossible without sensation'' (\textit{DA} III.3, 428b11--12). Accordingly, before we can analyze \textit{phantasia}, we must first address \textit{aisthēsis}, and before we can examine \textit{aisthēsis}, we must first establish the three ontological components that constitute all perceptual events: actuality, motion, and time. Their relationship is asymmetric. Actuality is prior in account and substance:\footnote{\textit{Metaphysica} IX.8, 1049b4--1050a16.} the potential is intelligible only through the actuality it is potential \textit{for}, and each motion takes its name from its terminus, not its starting point.\footnote{``motion takes its name rather from the goal than from the starting-point of change, ...'' (\textit{Physica} V.5, 229a24-25.} Motion is the fulfillment of a potential \textit{qua} potential\footnote{``the fulfilment of what is potentially, as such, is motion'' (\textit{Physica} III.1, 201a11--12.}---the ontological hinge connecting one completed actuality to the next. Time is the ``number of motion in respect of `before' and `after' (\textit{Physica} IV.11, 219b1)---derivative from motion and therefore doubly dependent on actuality. The chain's structural sequence proceeds chronologically, because that is how the ensouled body lives through the event. ``[W]e perceive movement and time together'' (IV.11, 219a4) as the passage from one completed state to the next; but, at each node, the completed actuality is what makes the preceding motion---and its temporal measure---intelligible. The list proceeds in the order of lived experience; but the explanatory priority is the reverse: each actuality is what makes the preceding motion---and its time---intelligible. Thus, you read the list forward; but, you \textit{understand} it backwards. 

Each motion in this chain possesses an identical internal structure, the schema of which Aristotle provides in \textit{De Anima}: 

\begin{adjustwidth}{0.5in}{0in}
    All movement involves three factors, (1) that which originates the movement, (2) that by means of which it originates it, and (3) that which is moved. The expression `that which originates the movement' is ambiguous: it may mean either something which itself is unmoved or that which at once moves and is moved. Here that which moves without itself being moved is the realizable good, that which at once moves and is moved is the faculty of appetite (for that which is moved is moved insofar as it desires, and appetite in the sense of actual appetite \textit{is} a kind of movement), while that which is in motion is the animal. The instrument which appetite employs to produce movement is bodily: hence the examination of it falls within the province of the functions common to body and soul. To state the matter summarily at present, that which is the instrument in the production of movement is to be found where a beginning and an end coincide as e.g. in a ball and socket joint; for there the convex and the concave sides are respectively an end and a beginning (that is why while the one remains at rest, the other is moved): they are separate in definition but not separable spatially. For everything is moved by pushing and pulling. (III.10, 433b12--26)
\end{adjustwidth}
Aristotle gives us three factors: (1) that which originates movement---the unmoved originator, i.e., the realizable good; (2) that by means of which it originates movement---the \textit{orektikon}, which ``at once moves and is moved''; and (3) that which is moved---the animal, via the bodily instrument (the joint as beginning-and-end). This is not merely a classification of physical locomotion. It is Aristotle's schema for \textit{any} psycho-somatic movement; and, crucially, the internal psychic transitions that constitute the chain (\textit{aisthēsis} \ra \textit{phantasia} \ra cognition \ra desire \ra action) are movements. It is Aristotle's canonlical articulation of a structural principle he takes to govern every motion that proceeds through a mediating instrument; and, the three-factor schema, therefore, supplies a structural principle that recurs at every level of the chain.

Aristotle establishes the universal nature of movement in the \textit{Physica}: ``all things that are in motion must be moved by something'' (VIII.4, 256a2--3).\footnote{For the full argument, see \textit{Physica} VIII.4-5, 254b7--258b9.} He, then, immediately specifies the two ways in which this can come about: ``[n]ow this may come about in either of two ways, either not because of the mover itself, but because of something else which moves the mover, or because of the mover itself'' (VIII.5, 256a4-6). The first disjunct names the moved mover---the intermediate that transmits motion through being itself moved by something prior (I trigger the first domino to fall, which knocks over the second, which, in turn, knocks over the third, etc.); the second names the unmoved originator---the source that moves without itself being moved in the relevant respect (a thirsty person sees a glass of water on the counter (realizable good) which sets the thirsty person in motion, but the glass itself is not moved by the desire it elicits). 

Aristotle deploys this structure at the cosmological scale in his discussion of the Prime Mover of \textit{Metaphysica}\footnote{\textit{Metaphysica} XII.7.} and \textit{Physica}\footnote{\textit{Physica} VIII.5-6.}. However, he also employs the structure with \textit{relative} unmoved movers: 
\begin{adjustwidth}{0.5in}{0in}
    And since that which is moved and moves is intermediate, there is a mover which moves without being moved, being eternal, substance, and actuality. And the object of desire and the object of thought move in this way: they move without being moved. The primary objects of desire and of thought are the same. For the apparent good is the object of appetite, and the real good is the primary object of wish. (\textit{Metaphysics} XII.7, 1072a24--28)
\end{adjustwidth}
The object of desire and the object of thought move \textit{in the same structural way} as the Prime Mover within their structural domains: they are unmoved movers in the strict sense just as the glass of water moves without being moved. The three-factor kinetic schema is, therefore, the universal structure of mediated motion instantiated at every scale at which such motion occurs, and with the unmoved originator's content varying by domain. At the cosmological scale: Prime Mover \ra celestial spheres \ra earthly motion.\footnote{\textit{Metaphysica} XII.8.} At the perceptual level: the sensible object \ra \textit{aisthētikon} (sense faculty) \ra perceiver.\footnote{``Sensation depends, as we have said, on a process of movement or affection from without, for it is held to be some sort of change of quality'' (\textit{DA} II.5, 416b33--35).} At the cognitive level: \textit{phantasma}\footnote{``But since when one thing has been set in motion another thing may be moved by it, and imagination is held to be a movement and to be impossible without sensation, i.e. to occur in beings that are percipient and to have for its content what can be perceived, and since movement may be produced by actual sensation and that movement is necessarily similar in character to the sensation itself, this movement cannot exist apart from sensation or in creatures that do not perceive, and its possessor does and undergoes many things in virtue of it, and it is true and false'' (\textit{DA} III.3, 428b10-17).} \ra \textit{nous} (thought) \ra cognitive actuality  (intellection, memory, discursive thought).\footnote{``We may admit to the full that being pained or please, or thinking, are movements (each of them a being moved), and that the movement is originated by the soul'' (\textit{DA} I.4, 408b5--7). } At the practical level: realizable good \ra \textit{orektikon} \ra animal.\footnote{\textit{De Anima} III.10, 433b12--26.} The three roles are formally identical; only the content of each role and the domain of the motion change. This is not accidental; rather, it reflects Aristotle's conviction that psychic events are a species of natural motion subject to the same structural principles as any other \textit{kinēsis} the natural world exhibits. Accordingly, at each level of motion within the actualization of desire's chain the three-factor schema is present, and something functions \textit{as} the unmoved originator relative to that particular motion-event. 

The actuality-first architecture and the three-factor schema interlock at a precise point: the completed actuality at each node is \textit{what serves as the relative unmoved originator of the next motion}. $A_0$---the dyadic first actuality of sensible object and sense-organ, situated within motion and time as the encompassing horizon---is the unmoved originator of the perceptual motion $M_0 \rightarrow A_1$, in which the sensible object's active potency and the sense-organ's receptive potency are jointly actualized. $A_1$ (completed perception together with the residual impressions it deposits) is the unmoved originator of the \textit{phantastic} motion $M_1 \rightarrow A_2$. $A_2$ (the \textit{phantasma} proper, crystallized by \textit{phantasia}) is the unmoved originator of the cognitive engagement $M_2 \rightarrow A_3$. $A_3$ (the completed cognitive actuality\footnote{The universal grasped, the past recalled, the possibility evaluated, the belief held, articulated by intellection, memory, deliberative/speculative thinking, the orthogonal committal dimension of \textit{doxa}, and the passions.}) is, when its content discloses a realizable good, the unmoved originator of the \textit{orektikon} motion $M_3 \rightarrow A_4$, through which \textit{orectic} action proceeds. $A_4$ (the completed action) is the chain's terminal actuality, achieved through the convergence of \textit{orexis} and bodily movement. Each completed actuality is both a terminus that \textit{names} the motion preceding it and an unmoved source that \textit{initiates} the motion following it. Motion takes its name from its end; the end, once achieved, becomes the beginning of what comes next. 

The chain of motion, therefore, is not a sequence of \textit{different kinds} of events but the \textit{same motion-structure} iterated at increasing levels of complexity. At each stage, the moved mover is both patient and agent\footnote{\textit{Physica} III.3, 202a21--28.}---the hinge that converts one motion into the next. The \textit{aisthētikon} is moved by the object and moves the organism. \textit{Phantasia} is moved by the residual impressions and moves the psycho-somatic state. The rational cognitive apparatus is moved by the \textit{phantasma} and moves the soul toward its determinate cognitive completion. The \textit{orektikon} is moved by the \textit{phantasma}-mediated realizable good and moves the body. Between each pair of hinges stands a completed actuality: the stable node from which the preceding motion receives its name and the succeeding motion receives its impetus. Time measures the passage from node to node---the before-and-after of each motion---and is itself intelligible only through the actualities that bound it. Each transition from one actuality to the next is itself a motion: a further actualization of a capacity that was latent in the preceding stage. $M_0 \rightarrow A_1$ is the actualization of \textit{aisthēsis} (made possible by $A_0$'s dyadic first actuality of sensible object and \textit{aisthētikon}, situated within motion and time as the encompassing horizon); $M_1 \rightarrow A_2$ is the actualization of the \textit{phantasma} (made possible by the residual impressions deposited at $A_1$); $M_2 \rightarrow A_3$ is the actualization of the completed cognitive actuality (made possible by the \textit{phantasma} as object of thought); and $M_3 \rightarrow A_4$ is the actualization of \textit{orectic} action (made possible by the completed cognitive actuality at $A_3$ that discloses a realizable good). Each transition is a motion in the \textit{energeia atelēs} sense\footnote{Here, I am using ``\textit{energeia atelēs}'' to describe motion as an actuality that is still on the way to its end as articulated at \textit{Physica} III.1, 201a10--14. As such, I understand \textit{energeia} as ``being-at-work.''}; each terminus is a completed actuality that becomes the unmoved originator of the next. 

Kenneth Burke's reading of Aristotle's \textit{entelechy}\footnote{Here, I understand \textit{entelechy} as ``having-the-end-within.''} provides an interpretive resource for understanding how the chain's iterated architecture licenses itself. Burke observes that the \textit{entelechy} introduces ``another kind of priority, namely the `principle' involved in a given form''---a priority that runs opposite to temporal sequence. A stage that follows another in time may be prior in form and substantiality: ``man is `prior' to boy because man has already attained its complete form whereas boy has not'' (\textit{Grammar} 261). The distinction Burke draws---between \textit{temporal priority} and \textit{priority in form and substantiality}---is crucial for the chain's structure. Each actuality in the chain stands in both relations to its neighbors. $A_0$ is temporally prior to $A_1$ (the encompassing horizon of motion and time must be in act, and the dyad of sensible object and sense-organ must hold their first-actuality capacities, before the joint perceptual exercise that produces $A_1$ can occur), but $A_1$ is formally prior to the specific motions it grounds, just as the completed form of man is formally prior to the boy who is becoming a man. The chain is, accordingly, not a linear sequence of self-sufficient stages but a nested architecture in which each actuality licenses and is licensed by others in a reciprocal relation of temporal and formal priority. This reciprocal structure is what makes the chain genuinely iterated rather than merely sequential: each node is simultaneously the terminus of what precedes it and the principle of what follows it, and each node's intelligibility depends on both relations at once.

Accordingly, Part I of my dissertation proceeds as follows. Section 3 (``Being-in-Motion-and-Time: The Ontological Horizon of Existence'') establishes motion and time as the encompassing horizon within which the actualization of desire chain unfolds: motion as \textit{energeia atelēs}, time as the countable number of motion, and the motion-time parallel that licenses the iterated chain structure. Section 4 (``$M_0 \rightarrow A_1$: The Actualization of \textit{Aisthēsis}'') grounds the preconditions (the object of sense and sense-organ) for the joint actualization of \textit{aisthēsis}, \textit{aisthēsis} as enmattered preservative \textit{alloiōsis}, the persisting residual motion (what I will call \textit{resonant kinēsis}) deposited at $A_1$, and the dual-tone thesis that specifies the internal structure of that residue (consisting of what I will call, respectively, \textit{resonant aisthēma} and \textit{resonant epithymia}). Section 5 (``$A_3$: Cognitive Actualities: The \textit{Phantasma}, Three Orientational Modes, and \textit{Doxa}'') develops the \textit{phantastic} motion $M_1 \rightarrow A_2$ traces the secondary actualization through which the \textit{resonant kinēsis} is taken up by \textit{phantasia} and constituted into the \textit{phantasma} proper, with its inheritance of the dual-tone structure. The cognitive engagement $M_2 \rightarrow A_3$ analyzes the cognitive engagements of the \textit{phantasma}---intellection addressing the image as bearing a universal, memory addressing it as likeness of the past, the synthetic mode of deliberative and speculative \textit{phantasia} addressing multiple \textit{phantasmata} as composable into unified objects of thought, and the orthogonal committal dimension of \textit{doxa} ratifying the aspectual presentation as propositional truth-claim. Section 6 (``Emotion: the Form of Desire Under Evaluative Disclosure'') treats emotion as the form \textit{orexis} takes when the completed cognitive actuality at $A_3$ ratifies evaluatively complex content: how the latent \textit{resonant epithymia} deposited at $A_1$ is articulationally-concretized into the passions\footnote{\textit{Pathos} in this dissertation names the most general structure of being-affected, in the manner of the \textit{Metaphysica} fourfold (V.21, 1022b15--21): the genus of any being's affectability, the structure of being-occurrable-to. This genus admits of, as I will argue later on, different levels of articulational-concretion: at the less-articulated pole, the hedonic tonality co-given with every \textit{aisthēsis} (a constitutive feature of \textit{aisthēsis}'s \textit{kritikon}); at the fully concretized pole, the emotions cataloged in the \textit{Rhetorica} (anger, fear, pity, shame, indignation, envy, emulation, kindness, friendship, and their kin) treated by Aristotle and Heidegger as constitutive of the \textit{being-in-the-world} of \textit{polis}-dwelling, speech-bearing animals. All of these elements will be treated in depth in their respective sections.} that mobilize \textit{orexis} into the species-specific \textit{orectic} form the particular passion calls forth, and how \textit{prohairesis} completes the \textit{orectic} structure when the realizable good is reached only through deliberation. Section 7 (``$A_4$: Four Types of Action and the Affective Architecture of \textit{Being-in-the-world}'') articulates the \textit{orektikon} motion $M_3 \rightarrow A_4$ as a four-type taxonomy keyed to the species of \textit{orexis} that mobilizes the bodily motion---\textit{epithymetic}, \textit{thymotic}, \textit{boulētic}, and \textit{prohairetic}---and traces how each completed action recursively reshapes the dispositional ground (the \textit{hexeis}) from which subsequent actualizations of the chain proceed, constitute the affective architecture of \textit{being-in-the-world}. 


\inlinenote{May need to revise the prohairesis sentence if i move that to the final section.}

The actualization chain begins not with perception but with the ontological conditions perception presupposes. $A_0$ designates the chain's first actuality: the dyadic structure of the sensible object and the \textit{aisthētikon} in their conjoint actuality, the perceiver-perceptible pair from which the chain's opening motion ($M_0 \rightarrow A_1$) proceeds. This first actuality is itself situated within an encompassing horizon---motion (\textit{kinēsis}) and time (\textit{chronos}), the co-implicated structures whose prior actuality is the necessary condition for any chain-actualization to proceed. Before sensible objects can exercise their sensible powers, before sense-organs can hold first actuality as capacity, before the rational soul can actualize the counting through which temporal succession becomes articulated, motion and time must already be in act. The chain cannot begin with a static potentiality awaiting external awakening; it must begin with actualities that are themselves active---both the encompassing horizon of motion and time and, against this horizon, the dyadic actuality of $A_0$ itself. The section's notation is settled: $A_n$ names actualization stages (nodes), and $M_n \rightarrow A_{n+1}$ names the transitions between them (the motion-subscript indexes the source actuality; the terminus is given by the preceding actuality. The chain $A_0 \rightarrow A_1 \rightarrow A_2 \rightarrow A_3 \rightarrow A_4$ runs from this first actuality through \textit{aisthēsis} (perception), \textit{phantasia}, articulated desire, and the \textit{orektikon} transition to overt action.

The section's architecture must be read against its twofold project. The first is to demonstrate \textit{phantasia} as the soul's unifying capacity---that through which past (memory), present (perception), and future (anticipation) are held together as a single cohesive temporal narrative, without which no extended deliberation, no rational action, no emotion, and no continuous self-understanding would be possible. The second is to fully articulate the ontological structure behind the actualization of \textit{orexis}---the perception-to-action sequence---in its two registers: synchronically, as the iterated chain that unfolds within a discrete event from first perceptual encounter to completed action; and diachronically, as the cumulative trajectory within being-in-the-world, where each completed action reshapes the dispositional ground (\textit{hexeis} chief among them) from which subsequent actualizations proceed. Because this two-register articulation is the ontological structure of human being, it can serve equally as a methodology for analyzing any specific event from initial perception of stimuli through to completed action, or at minimum as a means of individuating the constitutive processes at work. The complete actualization chain the section develops is at once the perception-to-movement chain of \textit{De Motu Animalium}---\textit{aisthēsis} or \textit{noēsis} or \textit{phantasia} $\rightarrow$ \textit{orexis} $\rightarrow$ affections $\rightarrow$ organic parts $\rightarrow$ movement (702a17--20)---and the actualization chain of \textit{orexis} in which the source of all animal locomotion is articulated. These are not two parallel chains. They are one chain. Every cognitive act---including pure intellection---is itself a \textit{kinēsis} of the soul, since ``being pained or pleased, or thinking'' are themselves modes of motion of the ensouled being (\textit{De Anima} I.4, 408b5--7); and \textit{orexis} is the moved mover that produces all such motion: ``that which at once moves and is moved'' (\textit{De Anima} III.10, 433b17--18). The chain $A_0 \rightarrow A_4$ thus names one structure: $A_0$ is the dyadic structure of the sensible object and the \textit{aisthētikon}, situated within motion and time as the encompassing horizon within which all chain-actualizations unfold; the successive stages develop the single chain through which \textit{orexis} moves the animal via perception, \textit{phantasia}, evaluative articulation, and bodily preparation.\footnote{Nancy Struever's reading of Heidegger's lectures emphasizes precisely this ontological horizon as the \textit{Da-Charakter} of Dasein: ``\textit{kinesis} gives the authentic \textit{Da-Charakter} to Dasein, the vital place-time of Being. Thus, the ontological significance of the passions lies in our capacity for change, the \textit{Veränderlichkeit} of Being'' (109). The horizon is not external to Dasein's existential structure but is what Dasein primarily \textit{is}, in its capacity for being-altered, being-moved, being-affected.}
