Quoted passages preserve the translators' English verbatim; readers may encounter ``state-of-mind'' or ``attunement'' for \textit{Befindlichkeit}, or ``mood'' for \textit{Stimmung}, in quoted material.} which articulates the recursive disclosive character of attunement (\textit{Stimmung}), is reserved for the section on passion; for the present, the structural-readiness point suffices.

\inlinenote{I need to move this footnote to the first instance of a german word}

\inlinenote{``Attunements are \textit{not side-effects}, but are something which in advance determine our being with one another. It seems as though an attunement is in each case already there, so to speak, like an atmosphere in which we first immerse ourselves in each case and which then attunes us through and through. It does not merely seem so, it is so ... It is clear that attunements are not something merely at hand. They themselves are precisely a fundamental manner and fundamental way of being, indeed of being-there [\textit{Da-sein}], and this always directly includes being with one another. Attunments are ways of being-there of Da-sein, and thus ways of being-away'' (67). ADD TO WORKS CITED: Heidegger, Martin. The Fundamental Concepts of Metaphysics: World, Finitude, Solitude. Translated by William McNeill and Nicholas Walker, Indiana University Press, 1995.}

James Gibson's ecological approach to visual perception reformulates the medium-precondition in a contemporary register. Where Aristotle speaks of the diaphanous as transparent and capable of receiving and transmitting the form of color, Gibson speaks of the ambient optic array as a structured field of light that specifies the surfaces and layouts of the environment: ``Only insofar as ambient light has \textit{structure} does it specify the environment.\ldots\ The term that will be used to describe ambient light with structure is an \textit{ambient optic array}. This implies an arrangement of some sort, that is, a pattern, a texture, or a configuration. The array has to have parts'' (45); and ``the light specifies these surfaces, their composition, texture, color, and layout, their gross properties, not their atomic properties'' (46). In both accounts, perception is not a matter of bare stimulus impinging on a passive receptor but a structured encounter between an already-organized environment and an already-capacitated organism. The tension is real and worth marking: Gibson is anti-mediation, holding that the structured optic array directly specifies environmental affordances without representational intermediary; Aristotle's hylomorphism, by contrast, is a doctrine of formal mediation, in which the medium transmits the form sequentially. The Gibsonian parallel is therefore restricted: it holds at the level of structural affordances---the perceiver encounters an already-articulated environment, and the medium's structure is part of what makes that articulation available---but it does not extend to full convergence with the Aristotelian doctrine of form-reception. With this restriction in place, the parallel is genuine and structurally useful, since it supports Aristotle's insistence that the perceptual situation is not the impingement of inert stimulus on an isolated receptor but a structured encounter constituted by object, medium, and organ in coordinated first actuality. $A_1$'s \textit{dynamis}-side, accordingly, is not a single state but a constellation of preconditions, each in its proper first actuality before the perceptual motion $M_0 \rightarrow A_1$ can commence.\footnote{Daniel Gross's \textit{Uncomfortable Situations} extends the Gibsonian affordance framework into a humanities-side rhetorical-affordance theory that the present section's Aristotelian-hylomorphic reading anticipates: ``Rhetoric, as a humanistic form of affordance theory, posits a situation that is not neutral but is rather `persuasive,' threatening and promising with respect to our human being-in-the-world. More precisely rhetorical theory provides a set of awarenesses specially appropriate to human environments ...'' (Gross 93). The structured-encounter reading the present section develops of Aristotelian \textit{aisthēsis}---in which the perceiver encounters an already-articulated environment and the medium's structure is part of what makes that articulation available---is the cognitive-architectural condition for the kind of rhetorical-affordance dynamics Gross specifies at the contemporary register. Thomas Rickert's \textit{Ambient Rhetoric} supplies a third Heidegger-grounded extension that explicitly ties Gibsonian affordance to dwelling-as-attunement: Rhetorical ambiance stretches ``agency away from its traditional seat in subjects to a full consideration of action as \textit{material, affective, ecological}, and \textit{emergent}'' (129); ``the attunement to the world that characterizes dwelling is not passivity. Rather, it is activity that works through and with things as they show up in the world alongside us'' (224). The resulting tripartite frame---Gibsonian biological affordance, Gross's humanistic-rhetorical affordance, and Rickert's ambient-affordance---articulates at the contemporary register the structured-encounter ontology that the present section's Aristotelian-hylomorphic reading of \textit{aisthēsis} grounds at the cognitive-architectural level.}

\subsection{The Activity of Mover and Moved as One}

Aristotle's account of the perceptual act turns upon a principle inherited from the analysis of motion: the activity of the mover and the activity of the moved are one and the same activity, though they differ in their being. Aristotle states the principle at the motion-level: ``the actuality of that which has the power of causing motion is not other than the actuality of the movable; for it must be the fulfilment of \textit{both}.... Hence there is a single actuality of both alike'' (\textit{Physics} III.2, 202a13--20). The mover's \textit{poiēsis} (``making'', ``producing'') and the moved's \textit{pathēsis} (``being-affected'') are one in substrate, but different in being.''\footnote{Aristotle develops the same one-in-substrate-different-in-being structure at the level of agent-patient correlativity: ``there must be an actuality of the agent and of the patient. The one is agency and the other patiency; and the outcome and end of the one is an action, that of the other a passion'' (\textit{Physics} III.3, 202a21--24). The unity of substrate under different formal descriptions is illustrated through the Thebes-Athens analogy: ``the road from Thebes to Athens and the road from Athens to Thebes are the same'' (\textit{Physics} III.3, 202b13--14)---one road, two formal descriptions. The transliterations poiēsis (\gk{ποίησις}) and pathēsis (\gk{πάθησις}) for the correlative aspects are retained from the Greek; see Aristotle, Physica, ed. Ross, 202a13–25.}

\inlinenote{ADD TO WORKS CITED: Aristoteles. Physica. Edited by W. D. Ross, Oxford UP, 1950.}

Aristotle applies this principle directly to perception: ``[t]he activity of the sensible object and that of the sense is one and the same activity, and yet the distinction between their being remains'' (\textit{De Anima} III.2, 425b26--27). The sensible object's exercise of its power (\textit{poiēsis}) and the \textit{aisthētikon}'s reception of the form (\textit{pathēsis}) are not two events conjoined but one event under two \textit{logoi}. Aristotle illustrates with a striking example: ``a man may have hearing and yet not be hearing, and that which has a sound is not always sounding. But when that which can hear is actively hearing and that which can sound is sounding, then the actual hearing and the actual sound come about at the same time'' (\textit{De Anima} III.2, 425b28--426a1). The actual ``hearkening'' and the actual ``sounding'' are the same actuality under two descriptions (426a2); the sounding and the hearing arise together, in a single actualization.

The unity of mover and moved in the perceptual act establishes a fundamental principle of correlation: the sensible world and the sentient organism are not independent substances that happen to interact but correlative poles of a single actualization, each requiring the other for its full reality to be disclosed. Heidegger's existential reading sharpens this point in a different register. Dasein is not a static substance that subsequently enters into relations but a being constituted by its being-in-the-world; the receptivity that characterizes Dasein's being-in is not passivity but structured openness, in which what Dasein encounters is encountered as already articulated within a context of involvements: ``the totality of involvements which is constitutive for the ready-to-hand in its readiness-to-hand, is `earlier' than any single item of equipment'' (\textit{BT} 116).\footnote{Heidegger develops the relation between worldhood, equipment, and involvements: ``[w]hen an entity within-the-world has already been proximally freed for its Being, that Being is its ``involvement''.... Whenever something ready-to-hand has an involvement with it, \textit{what} involvement this is, has in each case been outlined in advance in the terms of the totality of involvements.... But the totality of involvements itself goes back ultimately to a ``towards-which'' in which ther is \textit{no} further involvement: this ``towards-which'' is not an entity with the kind of Being that belongs to what is ready-to-hand within a world; it is rather an entity whose Being is defined as Being-in-the-world, and to whose state of Being, worldhood itself belongs'' (\textit{BT} 116). Heidegger establishes that ready-to-hand entities are encountered as members of equipmental contexts articulated by chains of involvement, not as bare presences subsequently brought into relations of meaning or significance.}

As such, receptivity, on the existential reading no less than on the Aristotelian, is not the inertness of an isolated receptor awaiting contact; but the structural openness of a comporting being whose mode of existence is to be already disposed toward what it can receive. The \textit{aisthētikon}'s first actuality (the \textit{dynamis}-side of $A_1$) instantiates this structural openness at the level of perceptual capacity; Dasein's being-in-the-world generalizes the structure at the existential level.

\subsection{Hylomorphism and the Reception of Form}

The central---and one of the most controversial---claim of Aristotle's perceptual theory is that the sense faculty receives the sensible form without the matter:\footnote{The hylomorphic reception of sensible form without the matter (\textit{De Anima} II.12, 424a17--24) is among the most contested doctrines in Aristotle's psychology, and the literature divides along three broad lines. On the literalist reading, the sense-organ undergoes a genuine physiological alteration, literally taking on the perceptible quality (Sorabji 72). On the spiritualist reading, no material change occurs at all---perceiving simply \textit{is} an awareness of the sensible quality (Burnyeat 21-22). A third, intermediate reading rejects the disjunction: perception involves a physiological change, but the organ comes to share the \textit{logos} (proportion) of the quality rather than literally instantiating it (Caston 247).}
\begin{adjustwidth}{0.5in}{0in}
Generally, about all perception, we can say that a sense is what has the power of receiving into itself the sensible forms of things without the matter, in the way in which a piece of wax takes on the impress of a signet-ring without the iron or gold; what produces the impression is a signet of bronze or gold, but not \textit{qua} bronze or gold: in a similar way the sense is affected by what is coloured or flavoured or sounding not insofar as each is what it is, but insofar as it is of such and such a sort and according to its form. (\textit{De Anima} II.12, 424a18--24)
\end{adjustwidth}
To receive the form without the matter is to undergo a change that is formally but not materially identical with the quality of the object; the eye, in seeing red, does not become materially red in the way that a dye stains cloth, but it does take on the formal structure of redness. The ring impresses its engraved pattern into the wax; the wax takes the pattern; but the wax does not become iron or gold. What passes from ring to wax is the \textit{eidos} (``form'')---the structured design of the signet---not the substrate that bears the \textit{eidos}. Perception operates analogously: the color of the apple passes to the eye, the warmth of the flame to the skin, but the eye does not become apple-matter, nor the skin fire-matter. ``The sense and its organ are the same in fact but their essence is not the same'': the organ is a spatial magnitude, but the sensory power to perceive and the sense itself is ``a certain form or power in a magnitude'' (\textit{De Anima} II.12, 424a24--29).

\inlinenote{add the following sources to works cited: Sorabji, Richard. “Body and Soul in Aristotle.” Philosophy, vol. 49, no. 187, 1974, pp. 63–89. ||| Burnyeat, Myles F. “Is an Aristotelian Philosophy of Mind Still Credible? (A Draft).” Essays on Aristotle’s De Anima, edited by Martha C. Nussbaum and Amélie Oksenberg Rorty, Clarendon Press, 1995, pp. 15–26. ||| Caston, Victor. “The Spirit and the Letter: Aristotle on Perception.” Metaphysics, Soul, and Ethics in Ancient Thought: Themes from the Work of Richard Sorabji, edited by Ricardo Salles, Clarendon Press, 2005, pp. 245–320.}

Heidegger's reading of Aristotelian perception radicalizes this analysis structurally. Heidegger reads Aristotle's account of \textit{aisthēsis} as establishing that perception is itself a \textit{kritikon} (\gk{κριτικόν} `discerning, judging')---``a \gk{μέσον} [\textit{mesotēs} ``mean''] with the character of \gk{κριτικόν} [\textit{kritikon}], of the `ability-to-separate' one thing from another.... It is a being-positioned toward possible objects, which is a \gk{δύναμις} [\textit{dynamis} ``potentiality''] in the sense of \gk{κριτική} [\textit{kritikē} ``faculty of discernment'']'' (\textit{BCAP} 126). \textit{Aisthēsis} is not a passive imprinting but a discriminative event: the sense faculty, in receiving the form, simultaneously exercises its critical capacity to distinguish what it has received. The soul's capacity to discriminate---this is white and not black, sweet and not bitter, good and not bad, pursueable and not avoidable---presupposes that it has received the form of the sensible quality in a manner sufficiently determinate to ground such a judgment. 

Martha Nussbaum also notes the discriminating characteristic of \textit{aisthēsis}, but with one highly important difference. In her analysis of \textit{De Motu Animalium}, Nussbaum identifies the \textit{kritika} as Aristotle's ``blanket name for the cognitive faculties ... those that `sort' or `discriminate''' (269n70). These distinction-making or discerning faculties comprise three members: \textit{aisthēsis}, \textit{phantasia}, and \textit{nous} (232). The \textit{kritika} perform two essential roles in animal action: (1) they present to the animal some object of desire, and (2) they present the concrete situation as an example of what is or is not desired (232). Crucially, while any one of the three \textit{kritika} can operate independently in combination with \textit{orexis} to produce action, Aristotle's more careful analysis reveals that \textit{phantasia} plays a uniquely necessary role: ``\textit{[p]hantasia}, then, is involved in every action; it must `prepare' the desire whether or not actual perceiving is going on.'' It presents the object of perception or thought ``to the creature in such a way that it can be moved to act'' (233). 

Indeed, there seems to be an inextricable connection between \textit{phantasia}, \textit{orexis}, and \textit{aisthēsis}: ``inasmuch as an animal is capable of appetite it is capable of self-movement; it is not capable of appetite without possessing imagination; and all imagination is either calculative or sensitive'' (\textit{DA} III.10, 433b27-30). Or, as Aristotle states while discussing that even the segments of bisected insets contain both local movement and sensation, ``if sensation, necessarily also imagination and appetition; for where there is sensation, there is also pleasure and pain, and, where these, necessarily also desire'' (\textit{DA} II.2, 413b22-24). The most likely reading of this passage, as Nussbaum glosses, ``indicates that \textit{phantasia} and \textit{aisthēsis} are indeed coextensive, but [236] that \textit{phantasia} has some particular connection with apprehending the object of desire'' (236-237). This connection between \textit{phantasia} and \textit{orexis}, though, is taken even further by Aristotle: ``the faculty of imagination [\textit{phantastikon}] is identical with that of sense-perception, though the being of a faculty of imagination  is different from that of a faculty of sense-perception [\textit{aisthētikon}]'' and ``imagination is the movement set up by a sensory faculty when actually discharging its function'' (\textit{De Insomniis} 1, 459a15--17). Lastly, ``the faculty of appetite [\textit{orektikon}] and avoidance are not different, either from one another or from the faculty of sense-perception [\textit{aisthētikon}]; but their being \textit{is} different'' (\textit{DA} III.7, 431a12--13). 

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Major premise: Whatever has \textit{aisthēsis} has \textit{phantasia} and \textit{orexis} (\textit{DA} III.10, 433b27--30).

    \item Minor premise: Whatever has \textit{orexis} has \textit{phantasia} and self-movement (\textit{DA} II.2, 413b22--24). 

    \item Identity premise 1: The \textit{phantastikon} is the same faculty as the \textit{aisthētikon}, though different in being (\textit{DI} 1, 459a15--17). 

    \item Identity premise 2: The \textit{orektikon} is not different from the \textit{aisthētikon}, though different in being (\textit{DA} III.7, 431a12--13).

    \item Conclusion: Therefore, in the ensouled being, \textit{aisthēsis}, \textit{phantasia}, and \textit{orexis} converge as one underlying faculty---one in substrate, different in being---articulated under three aspects---perceptive, appearance-producing, and appetitive---and this convergence is what grounds animal self-movement. 
    
\end{enumerate}

Thus, it is likely, as Nussbaum concludes, that the \textit{phantastikon} and the \textit{aisthētikon} are ```the same' capacity, but `different in \textit{einai} [``being''],' and I would go a step further and include the \textit{orektikon} in this identification as well; the ``same physiological apparatus that is sufficient for one is sufficient for the other'' (236). Accordingly, as Aristotle makes clear, \textit{phantasia} is the movement resulting from the ``sensory faculty when actually discharging its function,''  and is thus concurrent with perceiving rather than posterior to it. Furthermore, as Aristotle claims, ``whatever has a sense has the capacity for pleasure and pain and therefore has pleasant and painful objects present to it, and wherever these are present, there is desire, for desire is the appetition of what is pleasant'' (\textit{DA} II.3, 414b4-6). In the perceptual act, \textit{phantasia}'s \textit{taking-as} is one event but two in being: \textit{phantasia} presents the object \textit{eidetically}---the round sphere \textit{as} an apple, articulating the bare sensible qualities (red, spherical, size, etc.) into a determinate this-\textit{is}---and it presents the same object \textit{orectically}, \textit{as} pleasant or painful, \textit{as} pursuable or avoidable. The object of \textit{orexis} is, in Aristotle's formula, either ``the real or the apparent good'' (\textit{DA} III.10, 433aa28); and since \textit{to appear-as} is \textit{phantasia}'s domain, the object's very \textit{appearing-as-good} is \textit{phantasia}'s doing. The perceptual act, in the moment of its exercise, is already a \textit{presenting-as}; the \textit{kritikon} that Heidegger locates in \textit{aisthēsis} is \textit{phantasia} at work within every act of perception. The hedonic tonality that Aristotle locates within perception itself---the object of sense as either pleasant or painful by which the soul pursues or avoids---is the \textit{orectic} face of \textit{phantasia}'s \textit{taking-as}, operative in the \textit{aisthētic} act. The single perceptual motion is one in substrate and manifold  in being: \textit{aisthēsis} as the reception of form, \textit{phantasia} as the presentation of that form under its aspects, and \textit{orexis}---in its perceptual species, \textit{epithymia} (``appetite'')---as the response the \textit{orectic} aspect solicits. The \textit{aisthētikon} is one with the \textit{phantastikon} as it is one with the \textit{orektikon}, differing from each other only in being; \textit{phantasia} is the middle term, the \textit{presenting-as} through direct perception with no interval of deliberation. Therefore, to receive a form during \textit{aisthēsis} is to register it \textit{as-something}, and to register a form \textit{as-something} is to register it \textit{as} an object of desire, \textit{as-something-pleasant-or-painful} which the soul then pursues or avoids which then moves the animal. This \textit{taking-a-form-as-something-pleasant-or-painful-to-be-pursued-or-avoided} is the provenance of \textit{phantasia}. The inextricability of \textit{phantasia} and \textit{orexis} is, therefore, the ground of perception's native motivational force: because the perceptual motion is already a \textit{phantasia}-presentation, it is already desire-laden, and \textit{epithymia} follows immediately upon the presenting of the sensible as good or bad. 

What \textit{phantasia} presents during \textit{aisthēsis} it also retains. The persisting impression that remains after the perceptual act is over is not the sense faculty itself but the form the faculty has received, sustained as a \textit{resonant kinesis} after the object has withdrawn and made available---as a \textit{phantasma}---for synthesis, recollection, and the application of thought. Frede's analysis of the cognitive role of \textit{phantasia} makes the relation between persistence and downstream cognition explicit: \textit{phantasia} has ``its role in the \textit{synthesis} and retention of sense-perceptions [with their hedonic tonality], and its role in applying \textit{thought} to objects of sense-perception'' (282). The dual resonance \textit{phantasia} preserves---the \textit{resonant aisthēma} and \textit{resonant epithymia}---is just the \textit{eidetic} and the \textit{orectic} aspect of \textit{phantasia}'s original \textit{taking-as}, now sustained as a structured residue retaining the formal and desirable character of the original perception. The consequence for the actualization chain is paramount: \textit{phantasia} does not enter the chain only at its second stage, with the persisting and re-presenting of the \textit{phantasma}; rather, it is already at work n the first, within perception's own discriminative activity. From the completed perceptual act onward, every stage is a stage of \textit{phantasia}---the \textit{taking-and-presenting-as} of perception, the retained \textit{phantasma}, the cognitive engagement of that \textit{phantasma}, and the desire it mobilizes---so that \textit{phantasia} is not one link in the chain but the connective tissue running through the whole of it. 

\inlinenote{Perhaps I need to swap the phrasing of \textit{phantasia's} function. No longer taking-as, adopt Heidegger's making-present but with the qualification: making-present-as. This encompasses both the taking-as, i take this round sphere as a baseball, and how that object is made present as an object of desire. }

\subsection{Perception as Enmattered \textit{Alloiōsis} and the Four Senses of \textit{Pathos}}

Although the reception of form without matter is the defining characteristic of perception, Aristotle is careful to insist that the reception is not a purely formal event detached from the body. Perception is an enmattered \textit{alloiōsis} (``alteration'', ``qualitative change''): a qualitative alteration that occurs in and through the material constitution of the sense organ. ``Sensation depends, as we have said, on a process of movement or affection from without, for it is held to be some sort of change of quality'' (\textit{De Anima} II.5, 416b33--34). The eye is physically affected by light; the ear by sound; the flesh is heated or cooled by the tangible object. These physiological changes are not mere accompaniments of perception but its material substrate without which the formal reception could not occur. The activities of the soul---including perception---are inseparable from the body whose form it is.

Two kinds of \textit{alloiōsis} must be distinguished. The deeper structural register here is \textit{paschein} (\gk{πάσχειν} ``being-affected''), under which Aristotle himself distinguishes two senses. The first is ordinary qualitative change, in which a substance's quality is altered by the imposition of a contrary: cold water becomes hot, a living body becomes diseased, a flame is extinguished. This kind of \textit{alloiōsis} is destructive; the contrary supplants what was there. The second kind of \textit{alloiōsis} is preservative. ``Also the expression `to be acted upon' has more than one meaning; it may mean either the extinction of one of two contraries by the other, or the maintenance of what is potential by the agency of what is actual and already like what is acted upon, as actual to potential'' (\textit{DA} II.5, 417b2--5).\footnote{What is translated as ``destructive'' and ``maintenance,'' respectively, are in the original Greek ``\gk{φθορά}'' (``\textit{phthora}'') and ``\gk{σωτηρία}'' (``\textit{sōtēria}'' ``preservation''). Additionally, what is translated as ``to be acted upon'' is \gk{τὸ πάσχειν} (\textit{tò páschein}) in the original Greek. I note this as I will be referring to the maintenace aspect of \textit{paschein} as ``preservative.'' See Aristotle, \textit{De Anima}, ed. Ross, 417b2--5.} The geometer learning geometry, the builder acquiring the art of building, the \textit{aisthētikon} receiving a sensible form: these are alterations that do not destroy the subject's prior state but actualize a potentiality already present as first actuality. The same structural point governs the perceptual \textit{alloiōsis}.\footnote{The preservative structure of \textit{paschein} that governs perception extends to the level of the passions: the determinate emotions of the \textit{Rhetorica} (anger, fear, pity, etc.) are themselves preservative actualizations of the soul's evaluative-orectic capacities rather than destructions of them. The parallel matters structurally: it secures that the affect of perception, of being-in-the-world, does not disappear but is available for further actualization at higher stages of the chain.} Perception belongs to the preservative kind. When the eye sees red, the eye is not destroyed or replaced by red; its capacity to see is actualized, and the actualization is a fulfillment of that capacity rather than its negation. The wax-signet analogy clarifies the point: the wax is altered by the ring---it acquires a pattern it did not previously have---but the alteration is not the wax's corruption; the wax is preserved as wax, and the pattern it receives is a determination of the wax rather than a substitution for it. 

What is at stake here is \textit{paschein} as the occuring of \textit{pathos}. If \textit{pathos} articulates the ontological structure of being-affected, then \textit{paschein} is the occurring of such being-affected, either as destructive alteration by an opposite or as the preservation in and through actualization. Aristotle clearly details the fourfold ontological structure of \textit{pathos} in his \textit{Metaphysics}:
\begin{adjustwidth}{0.5in}{0in}
We call an affection (1) a quality in respect of which a thing can be altered, e.g. white and black, sweet and bitter, heaviness and lightness, and all others of the kind. (2) The already actualized alterations. (3) Especially, injurious alterations and movements, and, above all, painful injuries. (4) Experiences pleasant or painful when on a large scale are called affections. (\textit{Metaphysics} V.21, 1022b15--21)
\end{adjustwidth}
Heidegger's reading of the fourfold in the \textit{Basic Concepts of Aristotelian Philosophy} gives each sense a substantive gloss:
\begin{adjustwidth}{0.5in}{0in}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item In its broadest sense, \textit{pathos} ``characterizes a being as something that can in some way be affected by something. Something can \textit{happen} to such a being.'' It is a determination of beings with the character or possibility of ``becoming-otherwise'' (\textit{BCAP} 131).
        
        \item \textit{Pathos} as the \gk{ἐνέργειαι} [\textit{energeiai} ``actualities''] of these alterations: ``the ``being-there'' of such a shifting occurring-to-one'' (\textit{BCAP} 131).
        
        \item \textit{Pathos}, narrowing further, ``as the occurring-to-one that has the character of the unpleasant, of the \gk{βλαβερόν} [\textit{blaberon} ``harmful'']. That which happens to me is harmful to me in its happening. This is, indeed, the way that we use the expression ``to happen.'' But \gk{πάθος} is defined still more precisely: harmfulness is related mostly to \gk{λύπη} [\textit{lypē} ``pain'', ``distress''], so that, as a result, \textit{my attunement to this occurring affects me}. It is a becoming-relevant of something, which aims at my attunement, a becoming-otherwise in the sense of becoming-depressed'' (\textit{BCAP} 131). The third meaning refines the second: it is not just any occurrence but the occurrence that, as Heidegger states later, ``tones life down'' (\textit{BCAP} 134).
        
        \item Fourth, \textit{pathos} as the magnitude of the harmful. ``\gk{πάθος} designates the ``size,'' the ``measure,'' of that which happens to me, that which occurs to me in a harmful way. We have a corresponding expression for that: ``that is a blow to me''' (\textit{BCAP} 132). The fourth meaning narrows still further: it is not just the harmful occurrence but the magnitude of harm---the gravity of the calamity that strikes.
        
    \end{enumerate}
\end{adjustwidth}

The progression is not arbitrary; it moves from the most general ontological structure (alterability) through the actual occurrence of alteration (\textit{energeia} of alteration), to the qualitative determination of that occurrence as harmful, to the magnitude of the harm. Heidegger's phenomenological assessment gathers the four under a single encompassing thesis: ``From these four meanings, the \textit{genuine relatedness} of \gk{πάθος} becomes visible; it is related to the \textit{being of living things}, which is characterized by a thus-finding-oneself-again-and-again. The occurring to one befalls and strikes one in this disposition'' (\textit{BCAP} 132). Taken together, the four senses articulate \textit{pathos} as the structure of \textit{being-occurrable-to} that belongs to the way of being of a finite living being. To live, in the Aristotelian sense Heidegger recovers, is to be ontologically structured as that to which something can happen, and, to which, in concrete factical existence, this and that, again and again, does happen. The occurrence, accordingly, need not without qualification have the character of the harmful; rather, ``Aristotle recognizes a \gk{μεταβολή} [\textit{metabolē} ``change, alteration''], \gk{κίνησις} [\textit{kinēsis} ``movement''], \gk{ἀλλοίωσις} [\textit{alloiōsis} ``alteration, qualitative change''], in which \gk{πάσχειν} [\textit{paschein} ``being-affected''] has the character of \gk{σωτηρία} [\textit{sōtēria} ``preservation'']'' (\textit{BCAP}, p.~132). There is not just one kind of \textit{paschein}: one is destruction by an opposite, the other is preservation by the actuality of what one was potentially. \textit{Pathos}, in its widest ontological reach, includes the structure by which a living being is brought into its proper actuality through its encounter with the world.

Each of the four senses is structurally satisfied at the perceptual level. (1) Alterability is the constitutive feature of the perceptual \textit{dynamis}: perception begins as a \textit{dynamis} requiring an external agent to actualize it (\textit{De Anima} II.5, 416b33--417a20). (2) Actual alterations are realized in the \textit{eidetic} and \textit{orectic} reception as the \textit{energeia} of the \textit{aisthētikon} in a perceptual event. (3) Harmful alterations are operative at the perceptual level both in the form of painful sensation\footnote{``Where there is sensation, there is also pleasure and pain'' (\textit{De Anima} II.2, 413b24).} and at the destructive limit where excess destroys the \textit{logos} (``ratio'') of \textit{aisthēsis}: excess sound deafens, excess light blinds.\footnote{Aristotle's point is that sense is a \textit{logos} or ration---a proportionate mean attuned to sensible contraries---so that perception occurs only where the sensible remains within a proper measure. Excess, by contrast, destroys sense because it overwhelms this proportion itself: what is too loud, too bright, too dark, etc. abolishes the ratio that makes sensing possible at all and is ``painful or destructive'' (\textit{De Anima} III.2, 426a27--b8)} (4) Magnitudes structure the perceptual register through Aristotle's doctrine that perception has a \textit{logos} or ratio, that excess destroys the ratio, and that stronger stimuli mask weaker stimuli\footnote{``For we must suppose that the stimuli, when equal, tend alike to efface one another, since no one stimulus results from them; while, if they are unequal, the stronger alone is distinctly perceptible'' (\textit{De Sensu} 7, 447b4--6).}---a magnitude-structure operative at the basic perceptual level.

Two consequences follow for the chain. First, the perceptual \textit{alloiōsis} is enmattered through and through: the formal reception is realized in and as the physiological alteration. Nussbaum notes the significance of this enmattered character when she observes that the activities of \textit{nous} in a living body ``must be linked with those of the faculty of \textit{phantasia}, which it would not be implausible to see as realized in some physiological occurrences'' (\textit{Phantasia} 238). If even \textit{noētic} activity requires physiological realization through \textit{phantasia}, then \textit{a fortiori} perception, which is the causal origin of \textit{phantasia}, must be thoroughly enmattered. Second, the preservative character of the perceptual \textit{paschein} ensures that the form received leaves a residue: the organ, having been formally altered, does not return to its pre-perceptual state the moment the object withdraws. Something persists.

\subsection{\textit{Resonant Kinēsis}: The Persisting Impression}

The completion of the perceptual act does not terminate the \textit{kinēsis} initiated by the sensible object; it deposits a persisting impression in the ensouled body. Aristotle makes the point with striking consistency. In \textit{De Anima} he observes that ``even when the sensible objects are gone the sensings and imaginings continue to exist in the sense-organs'' (\textit{De Anima} III.2, 425b23--26). In \textit{De Insomniis} he elaborates: ``The objects of sense-perception corresponding to each sensory organ produce sense-perception in us, and the affection due to their operation is present in the organs of sense not only when the perceptions are actualized, but even when they have departed'' (\textit{De Insomniis} 2, 459a24--28). The residual affection is not a metaphor for an after-effect but the continuation of the perceptual motion in the organ after the originating cause has withdrawn. Aristotle illustrates the mechanism with a physical analogy: ``that part which has been heated by something hot, heats the part next to it, and this propagates the affection onwards to the starting-point. This must therefore happen in sense-perception, since actual perceiving is a qualitative change'' (\textit{De Insomniis} 2, 459b1--6). The water, having been heated, does not instantaneously return to its pre-heated state when the fire is withdrawn; the warmth persists as a residual motion in the water's material substrate. So too in perception: the organ, having been formally altered, retains the alteration as a residual motion that persists for some time after the object has withdrawn. Aristotle gives a name to these residual affections: ``even when the external object of perception has departed, the impressions it has made persist, and are themselves objects of perception'' (\textit{De Insomniis} 2, 460b2--3). The Greek is \textit{aisthēmata} (\gk{αἰσθήματα})\footnote{Aristotle uses \gk{αἰσθήματα} (aisthēmata), rendered in this passage as ``impressions''; Parva Naturalia: A Revised Text with Introduction and Commentary, by Sir David Ross (Oxford: Clarendon Press, 1955), De insomniis 460b32–33.}---residual sense-impressions, the material of dreams, after-images, imagination, and the beginnings of memory.

\inlinenote{ADD TO WORKS CITED: Aristotle. Parva Naturalia: A Revised Text with Introduction and Commentary. By Sir David Ross, Clarendon Press, 1955.}

This section adopts the term \textit{resonant kinēsis} for the residual motion understood as a whole---the ongoing activity in the sensory apparatus that persists after the sensible object has withdrawn. The coinage marks two features of the trace that Aristotle's own lexicon does not consolidate into a single technical vocabulary. First, the trace is \textit{kinēsis}: a motion, not a static imprint, with the ongoing character of all Aristotelian \textit{kinēsis}. Second, it is resonant: it continues reverberating within the organ like sound continuing in a stilled bell, retaining the formal structure of the original motion while operating in the absence of its originating cause. White captures the reverberating character precisely: ``This lingering, resonating, echoing presence of sensible forms freed from their original matter appears to be the essence of \textit{phantasia} for Aristotle. The movement in the sense-power caused by the sensible object itself sets up a second movement which continues after the sensation has ceased and the object is gone'' (White, \textit{The Meaning of Phantasia in Aristotle's De Anima III, 3-8}, p.~498). The \textit{resonant kinēsis} is not the same in kind as the original perceptual act; it is a continuing exercise of the same formal structure, sustained by the organ's material substrate in the original cause's absence and it is constituted by two elements: the \textit{resonant aisthēma} and the \textit{resonant epithymia}\footnote{Caston reads \textit{phantasia} as ``an echoing of the initial stimulation in the sense organs: a side-effect, like the original stimulation in character, but unable to compete with fresh, incoming stimulations'' (Caston 47, citing \textit{De Insomniis} 2, 459a23--b23). The echoing-side-effect reading licenses the \textit{resonant kinēsis} coinage. Aristotle himself describes the residue as ``a feeble sort of sensation'' (\textit{Rhetoric} I.11, 1370a28), overridden by direct stimuli but persisting in the organ. The dual-resonance thesis generalizes the competition-with-fresh-stimuli structure to the residue's two formally distinct aspects: the formal-imagistic content (\textit{resonant aisthēma}) and the orectic-charge content (\textit{resonant epithymia}), each subject to the same dynamic competition with fresh stimuli, each available for subsequent re-engagement by \textit{phantasia}.}

The signet-ring analogy, extended to three phases, illuminates what the \textit{resonant kinēsis} adds to the hylomorphic account. (1) \textit{Potentiality}: The wax is pliable and smooth; the ring is held above. The sense-organ is ready and the sensible object nearby but unseen---$A_1$'s dynamis-side is complete, $A_1$'s actualization not yet underway. (2) \textit{Actualization}: The ring presses into the wax; the ring and wax are jointly actual in the impressing; the actual hearing and the actual sound come about at the same time. This is $A_1$ itself: the perceptual act as the shared actuality of sensible object and sense faculty. (3) \textit{Residual trace}: The ring is withdrawn, the actualization proper ends---but the wax has undergone preservative \textit{alloiōsis}, and the impression persists. The \textit{aisthēmata} remain. The \textit{resonant kinēsis} is the organ's continuing exercise of the form received, sustained in the object's absence by the organ's own material substrate. An alteration that vanished with its cause could not ground further actualities; a completed actuality that terminated in itself could not serve as the unmoved originator of the next motion. But the \textit{resonant kinēsis} persists, and in persisting it becomes the material upon which the \textit{phantastic} motion $M_1 \rightarrow A_2$ will operate. The character of that residue---whether it is a single undifferentiated impression or a structured composite---determines what the subsequent stages can generate from it. 

\subsection{The Dual-Resonance Thesis: \textit{Resonant Aisthēma} and \textit{Resonant Epithymia}}

The persisting impression deposited by the completed perceptual act is not a single, undifferentiated residue but a dual resonance comprising two distinct yet inseparable moments: the \textit{resonant aisthēma} (the persisting \textit{eidos} of the perceived thing) and the \textit{resonant epithymia} (the persisting appetitive valence). The \textit{resonant aisthēma} names the residual \textit{eidos}---what Heidegger describes, in his gloss on \textit{nous}, as ``the ``look'' of a being'' (\textit{BCAP} 135). A terminological distinction must be made explicit before we can move forward, specifically in regard to the difference between the actualization of \textit{aisthēsis} in the present, in the `now,' versus the residual impressions, the \textit{resonant kinēsis} that remains. Accordingly, I will use two related but non-synonymous pairs of terms, distinguished by temporality: \textit{aisthēma} and \textit{epithymia} name what is given in the `now,' in the present, whereas \textit{resonant aisthēma} and \textit{resonant epithymia} name the residual impressions that persist beyond that `now.' To reiterate, the \textit{kritikon} of the \textit{aisthēsis}-event co-gives the \textit{eidetic} content (the form, the ``what it is'') with the hedonic tonality (the \textit{epithymia}, the good/bad, pleasant/painful, pursue/avoid, the ``how it matters'') not as two separate events but as one event under two \textit{logoi}. Thus, on the one hand, my use of \textit{aisthēma} and \textit{resonant aisthēma} refers to the \textit{eidetic} content received in the perceptual act and which persists. On the other, my use of \textit{epithymia} and \textit{resonant epithymia} refers to the hedonic tonality received in the perceptual act and which persists. As I have previously noted, \textit{orexis} (desire) is the genus of which \textit{epithymia} (``appetite''), \textit{thymos} (``spirit''), and \textit{boulēsis} (``rational wish'') are the three species. I use \textit{epithymia} specifically rather than \textit{orexis}. For \textit{epithymia} is the species of \textit{orexis} whose \textit{orectic} aspect arises (rather than \textit{thymos} or \textit{boulēsis} because \textit{epithymia} alone requires no cognitive enrichment beyond what the hedonic tonality within the \textit{aisthēsis}-event supplies (the real or apparent good); it is an appetitive response aroused by what appears pleasant in the moment. \textit{Thymos}, by contrast, depends on an honor-laden appraisal of insult, status, or slight; and, \textit{boulēsis} is, perhaps, more cognitively demanding, because it depends on a reasoned, rational apprehension of a particular end as being good. Accordingly, the two pairs of terms are related as actuality to residue: the event experienced in the `now' and the \textit{resonant kinēsis} that event leaves behind.\footnote{Here I must note, that while Aristotle specifically mentions the kinēsis and the ``sensings and imaginings'' left behind from the actualization of perception, he does not specifically mention that any \textit{orectic} trace remains. This is my own extension of the residual \textit{kinēsis} he claims persists after the object of sense depart. However, it is not without textual ground. Indeed, in \textit{De Memoria} he describes how anger or fear remain after the event that produce such passions: ``[f]or a similar reason bursts of anger or fits of terror, when once they have excited such motions, are not at once allayed, even though the angry or terrified persons set up counter motions, but the passions continue to move them on, in the same direction as at first" (2, 453a26--29).}

Every perception is simultaneously a discrimination of a sensible quality and an evaluation of that quality as pleasant or painful, beneficial or harmful; accordingly, every perception deposits in the ensouled body both a formal trace of what was perceived and an affective trace of how what was perceived was evaluated.\footnote{Nussbaum reads \textit{phantasia} as the faculty that ``ties abstract thought to concrete perceptible objects or situations'' (Nussbaum 265). Caston extends the analysis to intentionality: ``[b]y making \textit{phantasia} (rather than belief) the common currency among mental states, Aristotle emphasizes a form of intentionality which is more basic than the conceptual, and firmly rooted in the general character of perceptual experience'' (Caston, ``Imagination'' 52). On these readings, the dual-resonance's formal and affective co-given character follows from \textit{phantasia}'s placement as the common pre-conceptual currency through which both cognitive and conative content are carried, rather than being an \textit{ad hoc} addition to the chain analysis.} The dual resonance is one in substrate---a single enmattered affection---and two in being, corresponding to the perceptual and appetitive faculties' distinct ways of being. 

As argued above in section 3.3, Aristotle does not treat \textit{aisthēsis} as a neutral reception of sensible qualities, but as already bound to pleasure and pain and, thus, to \textit{orexis} through \textit{phantasia}'s registering the form \textit{as} an object of desire.\footnote{``If any order of living things has the sensory, it must also have the appetitive; for appetite is the genus of which desire, passion, and wish are the species; now all animals have one sense at least, viz. touch, and whatever has a sense has the capacity for pleasure and pain and therefore has pleasant and painful objects present to it, and wherever these are present, there is desire, for desire is appetition of what is pleasant'' (\textit{De Anima} II.3, 414b1--6); later he adds, ``To perceive then is like bare asserting or thinking; but when the object is pleasant or painful, the soul makes a sort of affirmation or negation, and pursues or avoids the object. To feel pleasure or pain is to act with the sensitive mean towards what is good or bad as such. Both avoidance and appetite when actual are identical with this: the faculty of appetite and avoidance are not different, either from one another or from the faculty of sense-perception; but their being is different'' (\textit{De Anima} III.7, 431a8--14).} What matters here is the consequence of that structure for the \textit{resonant kinēsis} left by the perceptual act. If the \textit{pathos} of the perceptual act is one in substrate and differentiated in being as both \textit{aisthētic} and \textit{epithymetic}, then the \textit{resonant kinēsis} cannot be merely sensory: it must preserve both the \textit{eidetic} trace of what was perceived and the hedonic tonality under which it was given. Aristotle's enmattered accounts argument supports the inheritance directly. The affections of soul are ``enmattered accounts''\footnote{\textit{DA} I.1, 403a25: ``\gk{τὰ πάθη λόγοι ἔνυλοι εἰσίν}'' (\textit{ta pathē logoi enyloi eisin}), rendered as ``the affections are enmattered accounts''; Greek text in W. D. Ross, ed.} whose definition must combine formal and material aspects; the physicist gives the material account (a boiling of the blood around the heart), the dialectician the formal account (a desire for retaliation), and the full definition requires both.\footnote{For the full ``enmattered accounts'' argument, see \textit{De Anima} I.1, 403a25--b19.} If the \textit{resonant kinēsis} is an enmattered \textit{pathos}, and the \textit{pathos} has two formally distinct aspects (\textit{aisthētic} and \textit{epithymetic}), then the residue preserves both elements as dual aspects of a single material substrate.

Secondly, if the \textit{resonant kinēsis} were exhausted by its formal-\textit{eidetic} aspect---if what persisted were only the \textit{resonant aisthēma}---then mere retention of sensible form would suffice to originate pursuit and avoidance. The soul's taking-the-object-\textit{as}-a-bear would, by itself, move the animal to flee. But Aristotle insists that bare perception is ``like bare asserting or thinking''; it is only \textit{when the object is pleasant or painful} that the soul ``makes a sort of affirmation or negation, and pursues or avoids the object'' (\textit{De Anima} III.7, 431a8--14). The \textit{eidetic} content alone is motivationally inert. A distinct component---the hedonic valuation of the object as pleasant or painful---is required to initiate pursuit or avoidance; the source of all movement, as Aristotle states, is desire: ``that which originates movement must be specifically one, viz. the faculty of appetite [\textit{orektikon}] as such (or rather farthest back of all the object of that faculty; for it is it that itself remaining unmoved originates the movement by being apprehended in thought or imagination)'' for ``that which moves without itself being moved is the realizable good'' (\textit{DA} III.10, 433b10--15). The motivational autonomy of the \textit{epithymetic} trace is therefore not an ad hoc addition to the hylomorphic analysis but a structural requirement of Aristotle's own commitment to desire as the source of animal movement.

What the \textit{resonant epithymia} preserves is what this section names the \textit{hedonic tonality} of the perceptual event---the pleasant-or-painful aspect that is co-given with every perceiving and that persists as a dispositional trace after the object withdraws. The term is grounded in Heidegger's reading of Aristotelian \textit{hēdonē} (\gk{ἡδονή} `pleasure') and \textit{lypē} (\gk{λύπη} `pain, distress').\footnote{Heidegger insists that ``\gk{ἡδονή} [\textit{hēdonē}] is just like \gk{αἴσθησις ἐν τῷ νῦν} [\textit{aisthēsis en tōi nyn} ``perception in the now''], it is what it is ``in the moment,'' \gk{μὴ ἐν χρόνῳ}, ``not in time'' in the sense of a determinate span'' (\textit{BCAP} 164). While \textit{hēdonē} is itself a kind of \textit{kinēsis} ``insofar as it also has the determination of \gk{πάθος} [\textit{pathos}], the determination of the being-taken-at-the-moment,'' \textit{hēdonē} is, more accurately, characterized as a ``\textit{determination of the presentness of being-there as such}'' (164). Additionally, Heidegger insists that \textit{hēdonē} and \textit{lypē} are co-given with every mode of being-there: ``with every \gk{πάθος} (\textit{pathos}), but equally with every perceiving, every thinking, considering, with \gk{θεωρία} (\textit{theōria}), to the extent that they are basic modes of living, \gk{ἡδονή} (\textit{hēdonē}) is an inseparable companion'' (\textit{BCAP} 166).} Hedonic tonality, on this reading, is not an add-on to perception that arrives after the perceptual event is complete but a constitutive aspect of the perceptual event itself. The \textit{resonant epithymia} is the persisting tone of this constitutive aspect. What persists after perception is therefore not merely the \textit{resonant aisthēma} of the perceived quality but the \textit{resonant aisthēma} (\textit{eidetic} structure) together with the \textit{resonant epithymia} (hedonic tonality) under which the quality was apprehended.

The dual-resonance structure is the foundational warrant for treating the perception-to-movement chain and the actualization-of-desire chain as one chain rather than two. The chain runs through perception $\rightarrow$ \textit{phantasma} $\rightarrow$ desire-actualization not because two parallel chains have been collapsed into one but because perception itself, in its very structure, deposits both an \textit{eidetic} tone and an \textit{epithymetic} tone. The basic hedonic tonality is co-given with perception itself; there is no perceptual stage without a desire-component. The perception-to-movement chain \textit{is} the actualization-of-desire chain not by stipulation but because the perceptual \textit{epithymia} analytically entails the aspect of desire that drives subsequent action. That the perception-to-movement and actualization-of-desire chain are one and the same is not an external imposiiton, but, rather, the formal articulation of what the dual resonance structure establishes at its core: the \textit{eidetic} and \textit{epithymetic} elements of the perceptual event are one in substrate but different in being. That the two are one chain rather than two is therefore not a high-level claim imposed on the chain from outside but the structural articulation of what the dual-resonance thesis establishes at its core: the formal and appetitive moments of perception are one in substrate, and the chain that runs through both is one chain.

\subsection{$M_1 \rightarrow A_2$: \textit{Resonant Kinēsis} as Ground for the \textit{Phantasma}}

The completed perceptual act, together with the dual resonance it deposits, constitutes $A_1$: actualized perception and the ground upon which the next stage of actualization, $A_2$, will unfold. $A_1$ is not merely an endpoint but a threshold: it names the state of the ensouled body after perception has occurred, a state in which the organism carries within itself the \textit{eidetic} and \textit{epithymetic} determinations of its encounter with the sensible world. To reiterate, each completed actuality is simultaneously the terminus of its antecedent motion and the unmoved originator of its successor. $A_1$ is the terminus of the perceptual motion $M_0 \rightarrow A_1$; it is also the unmoved originator of the \textit{phantastic} motion $M_1 \rightarrow A_2$ through which the \textit{phantasma} proper will emerge. The \textit{resonant kinēsis} deposited at $A_1$ is the material on which \textit{phantasia} will operate at the next stage. The \textit{phantasma} is not created \textit{ex nihilo} from the sensible object's form; it is generated from the dual resonance $A_1$ has left behind. \textit{Phantasia}, as Aristotle defines it at \textit{De Anima} III.3, 428b10--17, is a \textit{kinēsis} arising from actual sensation and necessarily similar in character to the sensation from which it arose:
\begin{adjustwidth}{0.5in}{0in}
But since when one thing has been set in motion another thing may be moved by it, and imagination is held to be a movement and to be impossible without sensation, i.e. to occur in beings that are percipient and to have for its content what can be perceived, and since movement may be produced by actual sensation and that movement is necessarily similar in character to the sensation itself, this movement cannot exist apart from sensation or in creatures that do not perceive, and its possessor does and undergoes many things in virtue of it, and it is true and false. (\textit{De Anima} III.3, 428b10--17)
\end{adjustwidth}
The similarity is grounded in the \textit{resonant kinēsis}: the \textit{phantasma} inherits both the \textit{resonant aisthēma} (\textit{eidetic} aspect, making the object recognizable as a particular object) and the \textit{resonant epithymia} (the \textit{orectic} aspect, making the object matter to the perceiver) from the \textit{resonant kinēsis} deposited at $A_1$. Without the \textit{resonant aisthēma}, there would be no \textit{eidetic} structure for \textit{phantasia} to preserve, recombine, or transform; without the \textit{resonant epithymia}, the \textit{phantasma} would be a mere formal schema---a picture without significance---rather than the charged image that drives desire, aversion, and deliberation.

\textit{Phantasia} operates as the temporalizing mode of disclosure through which a finite, embodied, comporting being cognizes time as a coherent narrative of past, present, and future, and through which an object is \textit{presented-as-something-epithymetically-charged}: that is, as a determinate object bearing the hedonic tonality of the desirable or avoidable. It is the capacity through which memory, present perception, and anticipation are unified into a single phenomenal manifold. The \textit{resonant kinēsis} deposited at $A_1$ is the material on which that synthesizing capacity operates: the \textit{eidetic} and \textit{orectic} resonance from which such a manifold will be constructed by \textit{phantasia}'s synthetic activity. The dual nature of the \textit{resonant kinēsis} at $A_1$ ensures that whatever \textit{phantasia} takes up carries both the \textit{eidetic}-imagistic content needed for cognition and the \textit{orectic}-motivation charge needed for action. 

\section{$A_2 \rightarrow A_3$: Completed Cognitive Actuality and the Three Orientational Modes}

$A_1$, the threshold at which the perceptual act terminates, holds the \textit{resonant kinēsis} as a still-reverberating but indeterminate residue: a dual-toned motion, one in substrate and two in being, that carries the \textit{resonant aisthēma} (\textit{eidetic} structure) of the perceived particular together with the \textit{resonant epithymia} (hedonic tonality) under which it was given, yet carries them as a lingering echo rather than as a determinate object standing before the soul. The \textit{phantastic} motion $M_1 \rightarrow A_2$ is the actualization that converts this residue into such an object. \textit{Phantasia}---``that in virtue of which an image [\textit{phantasma}] arises for us'' (\textit{De Anima} III.3, 428a1--2)---takes up the reverberating dual resonance and gathers it into a determinate presentational image, crystallizing the \textit{resonant kinēsis} of $A_1$ into the \textit{phantasma} proper: a standing \textit{eidos} held in availability before the cognitive faculties. The \textit{phantasma} is not imposed upon the \textit{resonant kinēsis} from without; it is the \textit{resonant kinēsis} brought to determinate form, the same dual-toned motion now consolidated into an image that can be addressed as \textit{this} object rather than undergone as a diffuse reverberation. What the motion produces, and what thereby occupies $A_2$, is a completed actuality in the strict sense the chain requires: the terminus that \textit{names} the \textit{phantastic} motion preceding it and, in turn, the unmoved originator of the cognitive engagement $M_2 \rightarrow A_3$ that the present section traces. Because the \textit{phantasma} is drawn from a residue that was itself dual, it occupies $A_2$ not as a bare formal schema---a picture drained of significance---but as a charged image, an \textit{eidos} presented together with its hedonic tonality, a look that is at once intelligible and already mattering. It is this determinate, available, and affectively charged image, seated at $A_2$, that the three orientational modes---intellection, memory, and the discursive mode---take up, and upon which \textit{doxa} exercises its committal \textit{taking-as-true}.

Aristotle situates \textit{phantasia} as a capacity that stands between bare perception and rational thought, thereby making the transition from $A_2 \to A_3$ the hinge upon which the entire cognitive architecture turns. Indeed, as I will demonstrate, \textit{phantasia} occupies an structurally indispensable position insofar as it orders the transition from \textit{aisthēsis} to \textit{nous} (thought), furnishing the \textit{phantasmata} through which \textit{nous} and \textit{doxa} can engage what the perceptual act leaves behind. ``To the thinking soul,'' Aristotle argues, ``images serve as if they were contents of perception (and when it asserts or denies them to be good or bad it avoids or pursues them). That is why the soul never thinks without an image'' (\textit{DA} III.7, 15--17).\footnote{This claim is repeated later on, further emphasizing \textit{noēsis}'s structural dependence on \textit{phantasia}: ``[h]ence no one can learn or understand anything in the absence of sense, and when the mind is actively aware of anything it is necessarily aware of it along with an image; for images are like sensuous contents except in that they contain no matter'' (\textit{DA} III.8, 432a6-9).} Heidegger, similarly, reads Aristotle's account of perception as fundamentally a matter of \textit{paschein} (``being-affected''): the perceiver is affected by beings that show themselves in the world, in a relation of being-in-the-world already structured by disposition and orientation (\textit{BCAP} 265).\footnote{The passage extends Heidegger's \textit{paschein} reading from perception to thinking itself: \textit{noein} has the being-character of being-affected by what is discovered. This generalizes the structural pattern across the chain---the same disposedness that grounds \textit{aisthēsis} at $A_1$ extends into the cognitive operations at $A_3$, where \textit{nous} operates not as detached intellection but as a further mode of being-affected by what shows itself: ``Being-there as living, a being as being toward the world. Human being-there determined by \gk{νοῦς} [\textit{nous}]; discoveredness is in this \textit{being-supposing}. This supposing as basic mode of human being-in-the-world is ultimately also a perceiving-it, having-it-there-thus-and-so, i.e., of the world, not only determinate beings in determinate kinds of encounters, but \textit{becoming-affected} by all possible beings of the world (cf. \gk{ἡδονή---καταφάναι, ἀποφάναι---διά} [\textit{hēdonē — kataphanai, apophanai — dia} ``[pleasure---to bring to appearance, to show forth---through'']]). This \gk{νοεῖν} [\textit{noein}] has the being-character of becoming-affected by what is discovered, what, for its part, is only possible in such a way that this becoming-affected by what is discovered is grounded in a \textit{generally-being-discovered} and a \textit{being-discovered}, i.e., in a discovering, a giving-sight-of \textit{as such}'' (\textit{BCAP} 265).} The passage from $A_2 \to A_3$ thus marks the moment at which the soul's being-affected hardens into an \textit{eidetically} determinate and \textit{orectically} articulated presentation, a movement that is at once physiological and cognitive.

\textit{Phantasia} names the soul's capacity and activity of generating and presenting images from perceptual residue; it is the faculty ``that in virtue of which an image arises for us ... in virtue of which we discriminate and are either in error or not (\textit{De Anima} III.3, 428a1-3). The \textit{phantasma}, on the other hand, names the determinate image that results from that activity. This distinction is well articulated in secondary literature,\footnote{Frede argues that \textit{phantasia} arises from sensation but is not reducible to it, with \textit{phantasmata} functioning as the cognitive vehicle of all post-sensory and non-perceptual thought: ``all \textit{phantasiai} are motions in the soul caused by sense-perceptions. They are sensory images or imprint that can exist independently from their original source'' (294); \textit{phantasia} accordingly ``plays a crucial cognitive role both in practical and in theoretical thinking in Aristotle by supplying the necessary link between the sensible and the intelligible'' (292). Papachristou refines the picture by distinguishing \textit{three} kinds or grades of \textit{phantasia}: ``Indefinite/Indeterminate \textit{Phantasia} (\gk{Ἀόριστος Φαντασία})'' (33), ``Sensitive \textit{Phantasia} (\gk{Αἰσθητικὴ Φαντασία})'' (35), and ``Calculative or Deliberative \textit{Phantasia} (\gk{Λογιστικὴ } or \gk{Βουλευτικὴ Φαντασία})'' (41)---corresponding respectively to imperfect animals, perfect animals with multiple senses, and rational animals.} and is essential for understanding how Aristotle's cognitive psychology operates. Aristotle's account requires us to recognize that \textit{phantasia} admits of internal grades: specifically, the distinction between sensory \textit{phantasia}, which operates in all animals possessing perception, and deliberative \textit{phantasia}, which belongs only to rational animals capable of calculation and planning.\footnote{In \textit{De Anima}, Aristotle distinguishes the two higher kinds of \textit{phantasia}: ``all imagination is either calculative or sensitive'' (III.10, 433b29). ``Sensitive imagination,'' as he later states, ``is found in all animals, deliberative imagination only in those that are calculative: for whether this or that shall be enacted is already a task requiring calculation; and there must be a single standard to measure by, for that is pursued which is greater. It follows that what acts in this way must be able to make a unity out of several images'' (III.11, 434a6--9).} Sensory \textit{phantasia} generates \textit{phantasmata} that are direct descendants of perceptual episodes: each preserves the sensible form the \textit{aisthētikon} received without the matter, together with the hedonic tonality under which that form was given. \footnote{In \textit{De Anima}, Aristotle grounds the derivation: ``imagination is held to be a movement and to be impossible without sensation, i.e.\ to occur in beings that are percipient and to have for its content what can be perceived, and since movement may be produced by actual sensation and that movement is necessarily similar in character to the sensation itself, this movement cannot exist apart from sensation or in creatures that do not perceive'' (\textit{De Anima} III.3, 428b10--17).} Deliberative \textit{phantasia}, by contrast, synthesizes multiple \textit{phantasmata} into composite images that represent possible future states of affairs. \textit{Phantasia}, to reiterate, is the mode of disclosure that takes an object \textit{as-something} and so crystallizes the \textit{resonant kinēsis} into the \textit{phantasma}---the determinate image that then stands as the material upon which the cognitive faculties operate. 

The \textit{phantasma} proper, as constituted at $A_2$, exhibits three essential characteristics that distinguish it from both the \textit{resonant kinēsis} (raw \textit{eidetic} and hedonic tonal residue) of $A_1$ and the conceptual content that serves as the foundation for intellection, memory, deliberative and speculative reasoning, and \textit{doxa} formation. First, the \textit{phantasma} is determinate: it possesses a definite formal structure that allows it to present a particular content to the mind, rather than remaining an inchoate sensory afterimage. Second, the \textit{phantasma} is available: it stands before the cognitive faculties as an object that can be contemplated, recalled, combined, or judged, thus serving as the common substrate for such cognitive operations. Third, the \textit{phantasma} is representational: it bears a structural likeness to the sensible particular from which it derives, and this likeness is what allows it to function both as an object of contemplation in its own right and as a sign pointing beyond itself to the thing it resembles.\footnote{The three characteristics---determinacy, availability, representationality---track three Aristotelian features articulated in \textit{De Anima}: (i) the \textit{phantasma}'s derivation from sensation as a \textit{kinēsis} (III.3, 428b13); (ii) its capacity for falsehood, where sensation is always true (III.3, 428a11--12; 428b17--18); (iii) its freedom from belief-conviction, since one can imagine fearful things without being affected (III.3, 427b21--24).} These three characteristics---determinacy, availability, and representationality---are not extrinsic features but follow from the essential nature of \textit{phantasia} itself as a movement resulting from actual sensation. 

Let's consider a real world example: You are walking through a park and you glimpse a particular dog---a golden retriever in a red collar---bounding across the grass and vanishing behind a hedge. In the instant it passes, your eye registers little more than a wash of golden motion, and for a moment after it is gone that impression lingers as an inchoate residue: the \textit{resonant kinēsis} of $A_1$, a fading reverberation not yet shaped into anything you could hold or inspect. What \textit{phantasia} crystallizes from that residue is the \textit{phantasma}---the determinate image of \textit{that dog}---and its three characteristics become legible in a single, ordinary act of mind. It is \textit{determinate}: the image has a definite form---a retriever's build, the gold coat, the red collar---and presents \textit{this} particular content rather than a vague golden smear. You could not hold the smear before your mind; you can hold the image, precisely because it has settled into a definite \textit{eidos}. It is \textit{available}: the dog is gone, yet its image stands ready to be worked upon---held in view, recalled an hour later, set beside the image of your own dog for comparison, or judged as to whether what passed was a retriever or a great dane; the same image is the one object on which contemplation, memory, combination, and judgment all operate. And it is \textit{representational}. Bearing a structural likeness to the dog from which it derives, the \textit{phantasma} works in two directions at once: as something you can contemplate in its own right, this golden collared shape, and, at the same time, as a sign pointing beyond itself to the animal it resembles, so that to entertain the image is already to be directed toward \textit{that dog}, the one now behind the hedge. None of this is yet the concept \textit{dog}. The universal that \textit{nous} abstracts from the image can apply to every dog alike---it wears no collar and is tied to no specific afternoon in the park---whereas the \textit{phantasma} remains the image of this one animal. The \textit{phantasma} thus sits between the two: more determinate than the inchoate residue beneath it, more particular than the abstract universal above it. 

Aristotle makes clear that the soul never thinks without a \textit{phantasma}, and this dependence of higher order cognitive functions upon the image presupposes that the image possesses sufficient formal determinacy to bear the intelligible structure on which such functions depend. Burke adds a complementary rhetorical perspective in his analysis of the traditional understanding of ``imagination'' when he notes William Hazlitt's use of \textit{identification} to account for the interaction of appetite and imagination when the motive of self-interest supersedes the interest of others.\footnote{``The only reason for my preferring my future interest to that of others must arise from my anticipating it with greater warmth of present imagination. It is this greater liveliness and force with which I can enter into my future feelings, that in a manner identifies them with my present being: and this notion of identity being once formed, the mind makes use of it to strengthen its habitual propensity, by giving to personal motives a reality and absolute truth which they can never have. Hence it has been inferred that my real substantial interest in anything must be derived from the impression of the object itself, as if that could have any sort of communication with my present feelings, or excite any interest in my mind, but by means of the imagination, which is naturally affected in a certain manner by the prospect of future good or evil'' (qtd. in \textit{RoM}, 83-84).} This insight, highlighted by Burke, reveals that the \textit{phantasma}'s representational character extends beyond the epistemological to the motivational: the image does not merely present content for contemplation but engages the \textit{orectic} dimension of the soul, moving the desire toward or away from what the image represents. 

Heidegger's phenomenological reading of \textit{De Anima} III.3–5\footnote{See \textit{BCAP} 132-137.} glosses \gk{φαντασία} (\textit{phantasia}) as a \gk{ποιεῖν παρόν}, a ``making-present'' of what has been perceived and holds it in availability for \gk{δόξα} (\textit{doxa}), memory, and deliberation:\footnote{``If, however, even \gk{νοεῖν} [the thorough deliberating of a matter, when I do not have it perceptually present] is something like a \gk{φαντασία} or cannot be without \gk{φαντασία}, then thinking too could not be without standing in the context of the entire life of a human being'' (\textit{BCAP} 134).} 
\begin{adjustwidth}{0.5in}{0in}
    \textit{Thinking}: this is not an appeal to a brain process, but to \gk{φαντασία}, the ``making-present-to-itself'' of the world, in which what is made present is not actually there, but instead is, say, in memory or in a merely faint making-present. Even in thinking about something, the matters are there in the making-present. \gk{Φαντασία} is the ground for \gk{νοεῖν}. Insofar as \gk{νόησις} [\textit{noēsis}] is the highest possibility for the being of human beings, the entire being of human beings is determined so that it must be apprehended as the \textit{bodily being-in-the-world} of human beings. (\textit{BCAP} 134)
\end{adjustwidth}
Heidegger's formulation rejects, in advance, the picture of \textit{phantasia} as image-formation in a private mental theater, and it correspondingly transforms what would otherwise look like three features of an inner mental image---determinacy, availability, representationality---into three structural moments of the soul's disclosedness toward the being-in-the-world as an embodied being. 

Determinacy is, on this reading, the \textit{eidos}-character of the look itself: \textit{phantasia}, through the \textit{phantasma}, presents a being as-such-and-such, and so can be true or false, because the look it has is the look of \textit{this} being and not another. Availability is the very ``making-present'' that grounds remembering, thinking, and deliberation---the furnishing of the \textit{there} in which a matter can be held before us when it is not perceptually present. And representationality is the \textit{uncovering} function of \textit{phantasia}: in bearing the same look as the sensible object, the \textit{phantasma} makes the thing itself manifest in a different, non-sensory mode---not merely epistemically, but as the mode in which a possible good or bad is made present \textit{as mattering for me}. What \textit{phantasia} makes present, in each of these moments, is the \textit{eidos}---the look of a being, the ``what it is''---and the hedonic tonality or \textit{resonant epithymia}---the ``how it matters to me.'' \textit{Phantasia} is, therefore, the light in which the look of something is seen and felt: ``[j]ust as it is through light that a color first comes to its being-there, is in its there insofar as it stands in illumination---being-there as the characteristic illumination, so \textit{every being that is there as a being requires a fundamental illumination in order to be there}.... This possibility is nothing other than \gk{νοῦς} [\textit{nous}]'' (135). The \textit{phantasma} is thus the way in which the \textit{eidos} and hedonic tonality of a being is taken up and held: structurally the same \textit{eidos} that perception first received, now held as the illuminated presence in which the thing itself can be seen and thought.

Because \textit{phantasia} is this illuminated holding-open of the \textit{eidos}, it grounds rather than merely accompanies the higher cognitive operations Heidegger names: thinking (``the matters are there in the making-present''), memory (recollection as a specific kind of making-present in which the ``having-once-experienced'' is also known as such), and deliberation (``the thorough deliberating of a matter, when I do not have it perceptually present''). This is the deeper sense of Aristotle's claim that ``the soul never thinks without an image'': not as ``psychic'' aspect of intellect devoid of ``bodily acts'' (\textit{BCAP} 134), but the indication that \textit{phantasia} is the bodily ground of \textit{noein} itself. $A_2$ is precisely the moment at which the soul holds the \textit{phantasma} of the perceived particular under the description ``image-for-the-mind'': the moment, that is, at which every higher cognitive act of human being-there shows itself as bodily through \textit{phantasia}, since ``the entire being of human beings is determined so that it must be apprehended as the \textit{bodily being-in-the-world} of human beings'' (134).

Accordingly, in the \textit{phantasma} we see what Heidegger calls the ``making-present''---or, what I will call, the \textit{taking-as}---function of \textit{phantasia}. Where bare \textit{aisthēsis} reveals proper sensibles only (white and spherical) the \textit{phantasma} is \textit{taken-as-baseball}.\footnote{Hawhee explicitly references Nussbaum and extends her position: \textit{phantasia} ``moves closer to the way Nussbaum (1985) figures it---as interpretation, but it is interpretation that is grafted onto direct perception: rhetorical vision depends upon both simultaneously'' (Hawhee 152). The grafting onto direct perception is what allows the \textit{phantasma} to bear hedonic tonality---to present the object as mattering. The \textit{taking-as-baseball} case is therefore not a cognitive add-on to a prior neutral perception but the structural mode in which the \textit{phantasma} discloses its content at all.} This \textit{taking-as} is \textit{pre-propositional}, but not \textit{pre-positional}. To further clarify this function, we can turn to one of the most important Aristotelian observations this section deploys throughout: ``I want to drink says appetite; this is drink, says sense or imagination or thought: straightaway I drink'' (\textit{De Motu Animalium} 7, 701a32-33). In this instance of the most basic \textit{epithymetic} action, Aristotle notes three different sources which can impel a living creature to move: sense (\textit{aisthēsis}), imagination (\textit{phantasia}), or thought (\textit{nous}).\footnote{Caston's reading of \textit{phantasia} as a distinct faculty further clarifies how the three sources operate uniformly: ``Phantasia thus stands midway between these different powers. It is more basic than the intellectual powers, extending throughout the animal kingdom, while also being capable of error.
